business situations. Such a process of benchmarking allows 
the system to gauge or recommend the best forecasting 
approach in such areas as accuracy, stability, and 
responsiveness to change.  
Such flexibility is necessary in situations where sudden 
shifts  in consumer  habits  or market situations  might otherwise 
violate  the reliability  of fixed models. The  novelty  feature  of 
the research  is in designing  a holistic  system  where  predictive 
analytics, inventory optimization [5], and financial planning 
are merged into a single, unified system. Rather than taking 
stock control and financial decisions as separate endeavors, 
the system  weaves  them  together  in an integral  way such that 
both operative  decisions  are informed  through  the help of  both 
internal  performance  data as well as external  financial  data.  It 
harmonizes  both the accounting  viewpoints  and the operative 
strategies so  as to strengthen decisions, build resilience, and 
instill medium - to long -term cost -effectiveness. Further, the 
in-built stock market forecasts provide a novel dimension 
rarely  seen in classic  inventory  management  system  designs, 
allowing organizations to pre -emptively align procurements 
with financial  opportunities  or threats.  The system's  main  aim 
is to provide  businesses  with a scalable  and intelligent  solution 
that not only accelerates forecasting precision but also 
advances financial alignment and operational agility.  
Through reducing uncertainties in both demand and cost 
profiles, the framework allows organizations to maintain 
service reliability while minimizing waste and inefficiency. 
The system's flexibility  in many  different industry  situations 
draws  attention  to its promise  as a transforming  tool for  supply 
chain  administration.  As world  markets  [6] continue  enduring 
volatility and unpredictability, such holistic and intelligent 
approaches  will increasingly  become  necessary.  It is towards 
such a vision that this research contributes through the 
provision of a practical data -driven framework bridging the 
analytics, operation, and financial strategy gap and thus 
facilitating  the evolution  of intelligent,  cost-efficient  business 
administration.  
This work is structured with the literature survey review 
given  in Section  II. Section  III outlines  the methodology,  with 
specific focus on its operationality. Results and discussions 
are in Section IV. Finally, Section V ends with the ultimate 
findings and recommendations.  
II. LITERATURE  SURVEY  
Inventory management was widely studied in various 
disciplines due to its pronounced impact on organizational 
efficiency and economic viability. Conventional approaches 
often emphasized economic order quantity models, safety 
stock  determination,  and heuristics;  yet, such  approaches  were 
seen to  lack flexibility  in dynamically  changing  markets. As 
big data  and machine  learning became salient,  recent studies 
explored predictive models for forecasting demands, 
automation in replenishment, and optimization frameworks 
for reducing costs. Additionally, the integration of financial 
planning in inventory  systems  attracted more  attention,  noting 
the importance  of synchronizing  decisions  on operations  with 
economic limitations. This review interprets developments, 
shortcomings, and voids in the available set of studies.  
This work examines the difficulties associated with 
procurement  and inventory  management within  construction 
projects, where  delays and cost overruns  frequently  occur. It 
presents a hybrid approach that integrates centralized 
oversight  with decentralized  execution,  thereby  ensuring  flexibility and responsiveness across various projects. The 
system prioritizes  real-time monitoring  and decision -making 
to optimize operations. By organizing [7] the flow of 
information and improving coordination, it connects 
traditional construction practices with contemporary digital 
solutions.  The framework  establishes  a basis  for Construction 
4.0, with the objective of minimizing inefficiencies and 
promoting adaptability. It provides a scalable and practical 
solution, poised for validation in prospective applications 
within the construction industry.  
This work explores ways to strengthen security and 
performance in cloud -inventory systems. Existing methods 
are often  ineffectual  in the presence  of dynamic  cyber  threats, 
hence endangering real -time data management. A novel 
methodology is introduced to strengthen confidentiality, 
integrity,  and reliability  in transactions.  The system  integrates 
high-level protection tailored [8] to efficiently regulate data 
processes, storage, and communication. It ensures safe 
transmission and protects delicate data from tampering or 
leakages. Further, the solution enhances system 
responsiveness through minimized delays and increased 
system  performance.  Results  from  simulations  demonstrate  its 
capability  to achieve optimal speed and security  in tune  with 
the primary needs of inventory operation processes.  
This work looks into the instability inherent in supply 
chain networks caused by time lags, which widen 
inefficiencies  and induce  the bullwhip  effect.  A mathematical 
approach  is applied  in the exploration  of the way fluctuations 
spread  through  networks  as delays  are changed.  Based  on the 
consideration of control strategies, the work reveals 
approaches  toward  suppressing  disruptions  [9] and enhancing 
stability  in supply  chains. Numerical  simulations  highlight  the 
effects  of delays  in inventory  decisions,  revealing  insights  into 
maintaining performance in the midst of uncertainties. The 
findings confirm that the introduced plan for control 
successfully suppresses oscillations, hence boosting 
resilience.  These  contributions  lead toward  the formulation  of 
more responsive supply chain networks capable of coping 
with fluctuating demand and time.  
The work investigates inventory management within a 
warehouse that is supplied by various vendors, each 
possessing distinct delivery schedules. It takes into account 
both predictable contractual demand and erratic customer 
demand, necessitating adaptable yet efficient ordering 
strategies. A reference model is implemented to guarantee 
precise tracking of stock levels while reducing storage 
requirements. This strategy [10] ensures that contractual 
commitments are  consistently  fulfilled while  retaining  reserve 
products to accommodate uncertain demand. Such an 
approach mitigates waste, facilitates timely replenishment, 
and strikes  a balance  between  costs  and the quality  of service. 
The system is designed to enable smooth daily operations, 
ensuring that excess stock does not accumulate while 
simultaneously maintaining readiness for fluctuations in 
customer demand across varying time frames.  
This work examines supply chains for fresh produce, 
focusing  on challenges  in perishability  and marketing  delays. 
Marketing delays may impact buying decisions, stockholds, 
and profitability.  Coordination  among  suppliers,  wholesalers, 
and logistics service suppliers in such a scenario is studied. 
Results indicate that marketing delays lengthen supply lead 
times and  are detrimental [11] to  the efficacy  of cost -sharing 
agreements. Common  strategies lessen  overstock situations,  Proceedings of the International Conference on Visual Analytics and Data Visualization (ICVADV-2026)
IEEE Xplore Part Number: CFP26VQ6-ART; ISBN: 979-8-3315-9237-0
979-8-3315-9237-0/26/$31.00 ©2026 IEEE 825
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:32:27 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
but extended delays dissipate collaboration efficiency. 
Optimal  pricing  and investment  decisions  are determined for 
different delay situations in the analysis. Results offer 
suggestions  on mitigating  losses  and enhancing  coordination 
in perishable commodity supply chains.  
This work examines pre -sale policies and pricing 
strategies  in the management  of perishables.  It also compares 
the determination of events  where  goods  are sold only  in the 
spot market with events where pre -sales are implemented. 
Since both price  and time  are determinants of demand, there 
is a need to balance both variables. It identifies situations 
where  pre-sale initiatives  enhance  profitability  and situations 
where spot sales are desirable. Using comparative analysis, 
the work underscores the sensitivity [12] of events to 
parameters of demand. Findings offer a more informed 
appreciation of why businesses can reduce waste, get more 
efficient, and  achieve higher  returns  in situations  where  they 
are working with perishable stocks.  
The work presents a production –inventory system 
blending sustainability considerations alongside monetary 
mechanisms. It highlights the growth in importance of 
regulations  on carbon emissions, compelling  industries  to turn 
toward  green  approaches.  The model  goes on to detail  today's 
payment schemes, where payments [13] often involve a 
mixture  of up-front,  fractional,  and credit  payments.  Both  the 
decay of raw materials and finished goods are considered, 
affecting replenishment strategies. A sensitivity analysis 
identifies  the impact  of payment  term and emissions  costs  on 
profitability  alongside  environmental  effect.  Results  are such 
that finding a balance in monetary arrangements alongside 
emission controls generates higher returns alongside a 
reduction  in carbon  production,  hence  synchronizing  industry 
aims and sustainability.  
This work presents a sophisticated retail system that 
revolutionizes traditional shopping experiences through the 
integration of real -time tracking and predictive inventory 
management. The system not only enhances customer 
convenience but also improves operational efficiency for 
retailers. Smart carts engage with the store's infrastructure, 
facilitating seamless billing  and precise  inventory  monitoring. 
Predictive algorithms forecast demand [14], which enables 
superior stock management and minimizes shortages. The 
combination  of edge  and cloud  computing  provides  a balance 
between immediate responsiveness and long -term customer 
profiling. Testing reveals enhancements in navigation, 
tracking, and overall satisfaction levels. The research 
illustrates how artificial intelligence can cultivate adaptive 
retail ecosystems, thereby benefiting both consumers and 
businesses through increased efficiency and personalized 
interactions.  
This work investigates electronics industry spare parts 
replenishment,  where  high-product  change  rates  make  supply 
chain  planning  difficult.  Producers  often  rely on bulk  buys  to 
cover  warranty  lifetimes;  however,  volatile  demand  threatens 
shortages  or costly  build -ups. It devises  forecasting  strategies 
for spare parts demand and purchase quantity optimization. 
Based on past sales and demand data [15], it increases 
confidence  in forecasting  usage  patterns. A control -theoretic 
approach adjusts buying habits to reduce errors from 
imprecision  in forecasts.  Case  studies  demonstrate  the strategy 
reduces inventory expense while providing product 
availability.  Its conclusions  highlight  an applicable  solution  for finding a balance between risk management and facility 
efficiency in after -sales service.  
This work performs an evaluation of omnichannel 
retailing operations, specifically  focusing on the  simultaneous 
impact of inventory controls and dynamic pricing. It sheds 
light on the vital aspect  of customer  experience  in determining 
optimal strategies for both online, offline, and blended sales 
channels. A dynamic optimization framework has been 
developed  to analyze  different  modes,  such as the buy-online - 
pickup -in-store  variants.  The report [16]  indicates  profitability 
and efficiency are very responsive to inventory states and 
channel availability. Where inventory is limited, fully 
integrated  modes  achieve superior performance. Additionally, 
the analysis  examines  the impact  of pricing,  service  level,  and 
customer  backflow  behavior  on the conclusions.  Results  from 
the work inform decisions in the dynamic environment of 
omnichannel retail settings.  
The work  investigates  the phenomenon of organizational 
change through intelligent systems for increased service 
administration. It achieves a multi -faceted architecture to 
integrate employees, objectives, and resources in a single 
intelligent entity. Real -time monitoring, data -driven 
reasoning, and predictive intelligence are applied in the 
system for increased resource allocation efficiency [17], 
where  drinking  water  provision  is the main  use. Web -enabled 
devices and AI -informed decisions lead to increased 
operational reliability  and performance. The strategy  supports 
increased user -digital engagement and hence  a collaborative 
organizational culture. It appears from the findings that 
intelligent  integration  not only increases  productivity  but also 
reduces  inefficiencies  and achieves sustainable  resource  use in 
harmony with organizational aims.  
This research addresses supply chain challenges in the 
operation of handling perishable goods in a set of different 
warehouses. The warehouses must meet both predictable 
contractual demand and stochastic consumer demand while 
allowing  for spoilage  during  storage  and transit.  A coordinated 
replenishment  strategy  is necessary  to guarantee  that the main 
warehouse adequately supplies [18] the facilities in a 
dependent  mode.  The work presents  trajectory -based methods 
developed to reduce complex system dynamics and assure 
demand  fulfillment  in each  node. Simulations  confirm  that the 
strategy proposed reduces inefficiencies, facilitates steady 
delivery, and accommodates aspects related to perishability. 
The report provides practical contributions for ensuring a 
balance  between  the cost-saving  aspect  and reliability  in large - 
scale perishable inventory networks.  
This research  creates  a tandem  queueing  inventory  system 
to simulate  practical  customer  service  situations.  It simulates 
interactions  in two stations, where customers  initially  search 
for product  information before  they buy. It  considers  service 
likelihoods, buffer capacities, replenishment policies, and 
possible server failures. Inventories are dynamically 
replenished  under  structured  policies  [19],  and the servers  can 
go on vacation  in idle periods.  Probabilities  and distributions 
are included for modeling  uncertainties  in arrival, service,  and 
repair times. Computational analysis offers sensitivity 
findings,  demonstrating  system  parameter  effects  on stability, 
efficiency, and customer movement. Findings provide 
directions for optimizing multi -stage service -inventory 
situations.  Proceedings of the International Conference on Visual Analytics and Data Visualization (ICVADV-2026)
IEEE Xplore Part Number: CFP26VQ6-ART; ISBN: 979-8-3315-9237-0
979-8-3315-9237-0/26/$31.00 ©2026 IEEE 826
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:32:27 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
This work  addresses  inventory  control  related  to reusable 
goods in a supply system, addressing both economic and 
environmental advantages of recovery practice. Constraints 
such as budgetary constraints, storage space, and ordering 
volumes are taken into account in the system, thereby 
reflecting practical operation challenges. Probabilistic 
considerations, ensuring robustness, are utilized to handle 
uncertainties concerning demand and supply. Metaheuristic 
methods are implemented for finding [20] near -optimal 
solutions in various scales and circumstances. Comparative 
results demonstrate superior performance, with a specific 
methodology consistently ensuring superior efficiency. It is 
ultimately determined from the exploration that efficient 
optimization  incorporates  both sustainability  and profitability 
and provides  a practical  guideline  for industries  dealing  with 
reusable goods under uncertain circumstances.  
III. METHODOLOGY  
Methodology describes the rigorous procedure followed 
for the  evolution  and verification of  the suggested  intelligent 
inventory  management  system.  It integrates  machine  learning 
approaches with accounting and market -driven intelligence 
for efficient demand forecasting, procurement optimization, 
and real-time replenishment. Designed carefully  at each phase 
to assure data integrity, predictive strength, and monetary 
alignment in fluctuating supply chains, the methodology 
progresses  from  data acquisition and preprocessing  to model 
implementation, stock exchange forecasting integration, 
autonomous replenishment enablement, and consequent 
model performance evaluation. It follows a systematic 
procedure  ensuring  the system  remains  scalable,  flexible,  and 
cost-effective while overcoming inefficiencies inherent in 
customary inventory handling practice (Fig.1) . 
 
Fig. 1: System  Architecture  
A. Data  Acquisition  
The foundation of the system is predicated on the 
acquisition of comprehensive and diverse datasets that 
encapsulate  both the  operational  and financial  dimensions  of 
inventory  management. Historical sales  records  are gathered 
to elucidate consumer purchasing behavior and seasonal 
variations. Accounting logs are amalgamated to furnish 
information pertaining to product costs, profit margins, and 
transaction history. Inventory logs meticulously document 
stock  levels,  inflow,  and outflow  dynamics,  thereby  ensuring 
operational tracking. Furthermore, external stock market 
indices  are procured  to facilitate  the analysis of  price  volatility 
and procurement risks. Collectively, these data sources are 
harmonized into a central repository, establishing a robust 
foundation for the development of predictive models that 
accurately reflect real -world conditions.  B. Data  Preprocessing  
Following the  collection of data, preprocessing  guarantees 
uniformity, reliability, and preparedness for the 
implementation of  machine  learning.  Typically, raw  datasets 
contain inconsistencies such as missing values, duplicates, 
and noise, which are rectified through the application of 
cleaning  techniques.  Normalization  adjusts  attributes  such as 
sales, costs, and financial ratios to ensure they fall within 
comparable ranges. Time -series decomposition is utilized to 
distinguish  long-term demand  trends  from  seasonal  variations. 
Feature  engineering  generates  lag variables,  moving  averages, 
and ratio-based indicators that enhance predictive modeling. 
Dimensionality reduction removes redundant correlations, 
thereby improving computational efficiency. Consequently, 
the refined dataset offers high -quality, structured inputs to 
machine learning algorithms, thereby ensuring that 
subsequent forecasting models function with accuracy and 
minimized error propagation.  
C. Model  Implementation  
The main predictive system is run in MATLAB, where 
LGBMRegressor  is chosen  as the main forecasting  engine.  It 
is trained  on preprocessed  data and tuned  via hyperparameter 
optimization in order to enhance prediction efficiency and 
scalability.  As a performance  baseline,  further  models  such as 
Facebook Prophet, LSTM, CNN, and a CNN -LSTM hybrid 
are run on the same set of data. Standardized training and 
testing  protocols  are applied  to each of  them, ensuring  thereby 
a comparability of methods. Greatest stress is put on 
determining how each method adapts to different demand 
patterns, seasonality, and price fluctuations. Such a 
comparative  study  assures  us of the chosen framework  being 
the most robust and computationally efficient solution for 
inventory  prediction.  
D. Stock  Market  Integration  
A distinctive component of  the system is  the incorporation 
of stock market forecasting to guide procurement decisions. 
Historical stock price data, market indices, and volatility 
indicators  are examined  to anticipate future  cost movements. 
The forecasting module offers insights into potential 
alterations  in procurement  prices,  which  are taken  into account 
during inventory planning. This approach guarantees that 
replenishment strategies  are not solely  driven by  demand  but 
are also financially optimized. By correlating operational 
decisions  with market  trends,  the system reduces  procurement 
risks and aligns with overarching financial planning 
objectives. This integration represents a significant 
advancement, connecting supply chain operations with real - 
time financial intelligence.  
E. Real-Time  Monitoring  and Replenishment  
In order to sustain continuous operational efficiency, the 
system integrates real -time monitoring of stock levels. 
Inventory data is dynamically updated and juxtaposed with 
projected demand. Whenever discrepancies arise, such as 
potential shortages or excessive holding levels, the system 
autonomously generates replenishment triggers. These 
triggers initiate procurement or adjustment actions, thereby 
diminishing delays and minimizing manual oversight. 
Additionally,  the process  is directed  by stock  market  forecasts, 
ensuring that replenishment aligns with cost -effective 
strategies. This mechanism mitigates stockouts, minimizes 
overstock   risks,   and  enhances   financial   stability.  Proceedings of the International Conference on Visual Analytics and Data Visualization (ICVADV-2026)
IEEE Xplore Part Number: CFP26VQ6-ART; ISBN: 979-8-3315-9237-0
979-8-3315-9237-0/26/$31.00 ©2026 IEEE 827
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:32:27 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
Consequently, real -time automation furnishes  businesses  with 
a self -regulating inventory system adept at adapting to 
fluctuating demand and market conditions.  
F. Model  Evaluation  
Predictive framework performance is assessed via a 
stringent  model  evaluation.  A host of performance  measures, 
such as accuracy, mean squared error, and efficiency, are 
applied to measure reliability. Comparative testing for 
LGBMRegressor and rival models indicates differences in 
flexibility in adapting to fluctuating data situations. Cross - 
validation methods are applied to guarantee models remain 
consistent for different datasets and different time horizons. 
Error analysis is  conducted  to investigate  areas  of discrepancy 
and promote  robustness.  It is found  that the holistic  approach 
outperforms customary inventory practice significantly 
through increased forecasting capability, flexibility, and 
monetary alignment, hence justifying the scalability of the 
system and extension to business scenarios.  
IV. RESULT  AND  DISCUSSION  
The experimental evaluation of the proposed smart 
inventory management system underscores its ability to 
enhance forecasting accuracy, financial alignment, and 
operational reliability. Utilizing MATLAB -based 
simulations, the LGBMRegressor model was trained and 
validated against a dataset encompassing historical sales, 
seasonal demand trends, cost fluctuations, and stock market 
indices. Throughout multiple forecasting cycles, the 
LGBMRegressor  attained  an accuracy  of 97.86%,  surpassing 
benchmark models including Facebook Prophet (93.24%), 
LSTM (95.42%), CNN (94.13%), and hybrid CNN -LSTM 
(96.57%)  (Fig.2) . 
 
Fig. 2: Model  accuracy  
The mean squared error for the LGBMRegressor  was 
recorded at 32.45, which is considerably lower than that of 
Prophet (58.77) and CNN (47.93), thereby confirming the 
model’s effectiveness in mitigating prediction errors. This 
outcome illustrates its proficiency in managing nonlinear 
interactions between sales and cost variables, thereby 
ensuring improved precision in demand forecasting (Fig.3) .  
 
Fig. 3: Mean  Squared  Error  
A fundamental strength of the framework resides in its 
financial integration. The stock market forecasting module 
attained an accuracy rate of 96.12%, which allowed the 
system to synchronize procurement timing with anticipated 
cost fluctuations. In scenarios where the stock market 
projected a 4.3%  increase in procurement prices, the system 
advised early replenishment to mitigate cost escalation. 
Conversely,  when  a 3.8%  reduction  in procurement  costs  was 
anticipated, the system strategically postponed orders, 
resulting in average cost savings of 6.5% across testing 
scenarios. This alignment of operational and financial 
strategies illustrates the system’s innovation in combining 
predictive analytics with accounting intelligence, thereby 
enhancing profitability and expenditure control.  
Real-time inventory  monitoring  demonstrated  a similarly 
significant  impact.  During  simulations,  the system  effectively 
identified potential shortages 5 to 7 days prior to the 
occurrence of stockouts. In 96.4% of these instances, 
automated replenishment was initiated, thereby averting 
service  disruptions. Likewise, when inventory  levels were  at 
risk of surpassing holding capacity, the system issued alerts 
with an accuracy of 95.7%, which mitigated unnecessary 
procurement  actions.  These  capabilities  markedly  diminished 
overstocking risks and resulted in an average reduction in 
holding costs of 8.2% compared to traditional manual 
methods. Furthermore, the automation led to a nearly 70% 
reduction in manual intervention, thereby highlighting the 
operational efficiency improvements realized through 
ongoing monitoring and adaptive control mechanisms.  
Benchmark comparisons showed further inroads into 
model behavior in the presence of seasonal fluctuations in 
demand and prices. LSTM did well in modeling sequential 
dependencies, realizing 95.42% accuracy, but had higher 
training times of 2.3 hours per seasonal cycle than 
LGBMRegressor's 38 minutes, rendering it impractical for 
real-time use. CNN realized 94.13% accuracy but did not 
perform well in the presence of anomalous seasonal 
fluctuations, while Prophet, although computationally 
efficient in modeling seasons, had lower flexibility in 
adapting  to nonlinear  market interactions  with a 6.76% error 
level. Combined  CNN -LSTM  capitalized on the  strengths  of 
both models, realizing 96.57% accuracy, but had high 
computational  demands,  with memory requirements higher  
Proceedings of the International Conference on Visual Analytics and Data Visualization (ICVADV-2026)
IEEE Xplore Part Number: CFP26VQ6-ART; ISBN: 979-8-3315-9237-0

than 1.4 GB per training  cycle  against  LGBMRegressor's  450 
MB per cycle. These experimental findings justify the 
selection of LGBMRegressor as the most practical and 
balanced solution for scalable inventory forecasting.  
Inclusion  of accounting  and cost-driven  characteristics  in 
the prediction pipeline brought real advantages in strategic 
planning. Through the combination of profit margin ratios 
and acquisition costs, the system offered output allowing 
businesses to  judge the financial feasibility  of replenishment 
strategies before implementation. Test results showed that 
companies employing the system achieved an average 
reduction in holding costs of 12.4%, in addition to a 9.7% 
reduction in stockout -related losses. Also, stock market 
forecasting -informed procurement decisions boosted overall 
financial efficiency by 10.2%. These quantitative results 
highlight the additional value gained from uniting working 
and financial intelligence in a single predictive framework.  
The analysis, in its entirety, affirms that the proposed 
smart inventory management system is notably effective in 
overcoming the limitations inherent in traditional practices. 
By attaining forecasting accuracy exceeding 97%, 
minimizing errors, aligning procurement with stock market 
predictions, and facilitating real -time automated 
replenishment, the framework presents a scalable, adaptive, 
and financially viable solution. The experimental results 
underscore its potential to mitigate inefficiencies, optimize 
expenditures,  and uphold  high standards  of customer  service. 
With its equilibrium of predictive accuracy, computational 
efficiency,  and financial  integration,  the system  illustrates  its 
preparedness  for extensive  deployment  within  contemporary 
business environments.  
V. CONCLUSION  
The research  provides  a holistic  framework  for intelligent 
inventory management, blending predictive analytics, 
accounting perspectives, and market -driven insights in a 
unified system for facilitating decisions. Through the 
integration of demand forecasting, budgetary plans, and 
current  stock  surveillance,  the system  proposed  addresses  the 
timeless challenges of supplying and demand balance, 
inefficiency minimization, and synchronizing acquisition 
strategy with budgetary ends. What is more, the integration 
of stock exchange forecasting adds a unique dimension of 
intelligence, thereby guaranteeing both operationally 
efficient and budget -wise replenishment decisions. Through 
extensive testing, the system has exhibited superior 
adaptability in comparison to conventional approaches, 
thereby demonstrating its capability to manage demand 
fluctuations, seasonal variations, and procurement 
uncertainties.  The framework  serves  to effectively  bridge  the 
divide between operational logistics and financial planning, 
thus providing businesses with a strategic advantage in 
dynamic and competitive environments. Its capacity to 
initiate  automated  replenishment,  reduce  human  intervention, 
and facilitate continuous monitoring establishes it as a 
dependable and scalable solution for contemporary supply 
chains. Lastly, the proposed intelligent inventory system 
constitutes a significant stride in rational business 
procedures, guaranteeing greater efficiency, economic 
benefits, and strategic compatibility.  REFERENCES

Enhanced sales forecasting of perishable and 
non- perishable goods using weather data with 
insights on Imports and price  sensitivity  
 
 
Lalitha R 
Professor,  CSE 
Rajalakshmi  Institute  of Technology 
Chennai, India . 
researchlalitha@gmail.com   
 
Sreelekha Ponugoti

                     Hanne Jenifer R  
CSE

Rajalakshmi  Institute  of Technology

 dhanu72005@gmail.com
 
 
Abstract : In the competitive restaurant industry, success 
hinges on accurate demand forecasting and optimal pricing 
strategies. Weather factors such as temperature, rain, and 
seasonal changes significantly impact consumer behavior, 
particularly affecting sales of goods categorized as highly 
perishable (e.g., fresh produce, dairy), perishable (e.g., bread, 
meat),  and non-perishable  (e.g.,  canned  goods,  dry ingredients). 
The project leverages historical weather patterns, ingredient 
prices, and machine learning to forecast sales and implement 
dynamic pricing, with a focus on import status and price 
sensitivity. Using Random Forest and ARIMA models, the 
system conducts time series analysis for precise demand 
prediction. Built with Python and utilizing libraries like scikit - 
learn and pandas, the project processes real -time weather and 
ingredient price data to enable timely price adjustments and 
inventory management. The data -driven approach empowers 
restaurants to maximize revenue, optimize menu pricing, and 
enhance customer satisfaction in a fluctuating market.  
Keywords  --- Demand  forecasting,  dynamic  pricing,  weather 
data, machine learning, time series analysis, Random Forest, 
ARIMA, highly perishable goods, perishable goods, non - 
perishable goods, import status, price sensitivity, restaurant 
sales, real -time data.  
I.  INTRODUCTION  
This fast -growing restaurant environment is forcing the 
operations to be always better in knowing the customer and 
predicting his behavior and decision -making. Two forces 
which were minor  factors relatively, weather and ingredient 
prices, have now become major contributors to restaurant 
sales. Weather can decide when and how frequently 
customers go out to dine, for favorable conditions boost 
dining  out, while  unfavorable  weather  hinders  it. Meanwhile, 
fluctuations in the  price  of ingredients  pressure  the margins, 
and thus strategic  changes  have  to be made  in order to stay 
profitable as well as deliver customer satisfaction.  
Of major challenge, restaurant industry is faced with 
optimizing  sales  of perishable  and non-perishable  as well as 
import  goods  due to dynamic  factors like weather, demand  patterns, and price sensitivity. Traditional models cannot 
give accurate demand forecasting; instead, product 
overstocking  or stockouts,  which  is a significant  problem  for 
perishable products with low shelf lives, occurred. This 
project will erase all those problems by using machine 
learning algorithms like Time Series Forecasting and 
Dynamic  Pricing  in predicting  the amounts  of demand  based 
on local weather forecast data and types of product 
(perishable,  non-perishable,  and imported  goods),  ingredient 
costs, and other exogenous factors. The model returns an 
MSE value of [53.97], which yet again confirms that the 
model is highly accurate.  
 
 
Most conventional sales forecasting models do not account 
for real -time situational variables, such as weather and 
ingredient price fluctuations. This approach integrated 
various  sources  of data including,  but not limited  to historical 
sales, weather conditions, and fluctuating prices, thereby 
generating a  more  holistic  model.  The model shows  how the 
external drivers are influencing customer demand over time 
with accurate amalgamation of temperature, humidity, and 
rainfall data integrated with trends of price that could be 
influenced by market movements, supply chain disruptions, 
or seasonality. For instance, change in the weather can 
influence the demand for seasonal dishes, such as hot soups 
during winter or light meals in summer. This is something 
restaurants can plan their seasonal menus or promotions 
around.  
 
It develops  a dynamic pricing model that should change the 
prices  of foods  on the menu  according  to fluctuating  weather 
conditions and ingredient costs in real time, helping 
restaurants maintain profit margins even as they stay 
competitive -the tactic, more commonly employed in such 
industries as airlines and hotels, has been relatively new to 
restaurants. This model helps restaurants make smooth 
responses  to changes  in demand, ingredient  costs, and  other  
factors  going  into determining  their business;  therefore, 
profit margins will not be compromised at the cost of 
customer satisfaction.  2025 2nd International Conference on Research Methodologies in Knowledge Management, Artificial Intelligence and Telecommunication Engineering (RMKMATE) | 979-8-3315-9848-8/25/$31.00 ©2025 IEEE | DOI: 10.1109/RMKMATE64874.2025.11042488

Additionally, it can depict an overall view of whether 
weather conditions or price sensitivity affects sales so that 
informed decisions based on data can be made. Unlike its 
competitors that do not change when abrupt weather 
conditions or special events happen, the project will permit 
changes on real -time basis in order to minimize waste and 
maximize sales. This advantage will ensure accuracy in 
giving information to optimize everything in managing 
inventory,  reduce  costs,  and assure  satisfaction  of customers' 
needs. The dynamic pricing strategies, real -time data 
analysis,  and integration  of machine  learning  methods  would 
thus make the restaurant business highly adaptable and 
successful in this volatile market.  
 
 
The system will also facilitate the implementation of 
customer engagement strategies. For example, a restaurant 
can offer special  deals  for hot drinks  when it  is cold or  cool 
beverages when there is a heatwave. Targeted promotions 
increase  the level  of customer  involvement  and their loyalty, 
which increases repeat visits and cash flow generation.  
 
For these insights to be actionable, the project applies 
interactive  dashboards  with products  such as Power  BI. 
The interactive dashboards allow restaurant managers to 
understand  trends  and behavior  patterns  of customers  so 
that they  can make  proper  pricing  decisions  in attaining  
optimal operational efficiency. It gives restaurants an 
integrated  decision -support  system  in consolidated  
models  of weather conditions,  ingredient  prices,  and sales  
data for proper  sales predictions, cost management,  and 
customer  relationships.  
 
This model therefore addresses the weaknesses of the 
traditional  sales  forecasting  since  multiple  external  factors 
are all incorporated into one dynamic framework.  
 The advantage over inventory and cost  management  lies in 
its dynamic  pricing  and real-time adjustments,  so restaurants 
are confident about long -term success and continued 
profitability.  
 
The architecture diagram of the system is illustrated in 
Figure 1.  The system  has multiple  layers  aimed  at the 
integration  of data collection , model  development,  and 
decision -making processes:  
 
1. Data  Collection  : Various  sources  of data,  such as weather 
conditions, sales data, ingredients data, and price sensitivity 
are combined. The data is categorized into highly  
perishable, perishable, and non -perishable goods, thus 
offering insights into product demand patterns.  
 
2. Data Processing Layer  : This layer of data processing is 
aimed at making the processed data ready for analysis. For 
preprocessing purposes, this layer relies on relevant Python 
libraries like Pandas and Numpy and the tasks can include 
data cleansing, data imputation, and feature engineering 
among others aimed at extracting informative features for 
modeling.  
 
3. Model Development Layer : This core part includes 
models that would be based on learning machines for sales 
prediction, time series forecasting, and dynamic pricing. It's 
always trained on historical  data,  so the system  can predict  
future  trends  in sales  and change  its prices  accordingly.  
4. Data  Storage:  This section  will store  the processed  data 
for any further analysis needed for the next step.  
 
5. Visualization  : This layer  uses Matplotlib  and Seaborn  to 
form visual insights such as trend analysis.  
 
Figure 1: System Architecture  
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 10,2026 at 15:43:45 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
6. Automated Decision Making: The real-time prediction - 
based system can automatically adjust prices as well as 
provide  loyalty  programs  that award  customers  according  to 
their history of purchases, so more customers will get 
engaged.  
 
7. User  Interface  (UI):  Power  BI demonstrates  the processed 
information to the user on the dashboard. Restaurant 
managers can then track reports and change strategies 
according to new insights for that report.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 2: Workflow  
  
Figure 2 Overall Process Using a Workflow  How It Processes 
All Data, Including Collection up to the Adjustment  of 
Dynamic  Pricing  actual  implementation:  
 
1. Source  Data  Collection:  Weather  data,  prior  sales 
history,  ingredient  cost data,  and even  availability 
would be collected.  
2. Upload  Previous  Data:  Upload  prior  sales  data as 
well as other  relevant  data to be used for analysis  
3. Data Preprocessing: Cleaning up the data, proper 
treatment  of missing  entries,  and other  such which 
shall make the data of better quality for model 
training.  
4.  Train a sequence of Random Forest model in 
predicting the sales based on historical data and 
then follow  it by ARIMA  to predict  future  values 
of time -series.  
5.  Dynamic  Pricing  Model  Train  pricing  model  on 
the predictions  made  by sales  and the cost of the 
ingredients involved.  
6. Weather  Impact  Prediction  Generate  the forecast  as 
to how upcoming changes in the weather will 
influence sales.  
7.  Dynamic  Price  Adjustment  The prices  adjust 
automatically depending on the prediction of 
demand and the expected cost.  
8. Power  BI Dashboards: Visualizing  actionable 
insights on sales trend, pricing strategy, and 
customer loyalty for decision making.  
9. Automatic  Price  Updates:  Real-time adjustment  of 
food item price in the restaurant's price list.  
10. Continuous Adaptation: Continuously monitor 
incoming  sales  data and customer  reaction  to the 
system.  
It is an automated  workflow  that ensures  dynamic  and 
responsive  restaurant  sales  for maximum  profitability.  
 
 
II. LITERATURE SURVEY  
 
Literature work related to Weather  and Restaurant  Sales  
The influence of weather on the behavior of customers 
has been  described  and shown  by many  authors  in different 
contexts. For instance, Bujisic et al. [1] proved that the 
change in weather primarily affects the foot traffic into 
restaurants  as more  considerable  pleasant  weather  motivates 
customers to go out for eating. Similarly, Rose and Dolega  
[2] analyzed a spatial analysis to quantify the impact of 
weather on retail sales that is represented by the demand of 
having a weather -based model towards forecasting 
requirements.  Yang  et al. [3] focus  on incorporating  weather 
data into sales  prediction  models  by using  advanced  feature 
interaction learning mechanisms to include the impact of 
weather.  
 
Some of the studies include work by Tanizaki et al. [4] and 
Schepers et al. [5], which explores how diner behavior may 
be predicted using weather patterns, pointing out that 
comfort  foods  sell more  during  colder  weather,  while  lighter 
meals sell better during warmer weather. Nassibi et al. [6] 
argue that if restaurant chains include weather data in 
demand forecasting models, it will enable restaurants to 
understand their customers'  preferences  better, hence menu 
items are managed more appropriately.  
 Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 10,2026 at 15:43:45 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
Customer responsiveness is greatly enhanced in terms of 
response to dynamic  changes  in menu  items  with respect  to 
the weather, as  reviewed  from  Zháo  and Jayadi's  [7] demand  
forecasting for an Indonesian restaurant chain.  
 
Literature work related to Ingredient  Prices with  Dynamic  
Pricing  
One of the most significant challenges to restaurant 
profitability is dynamic ingredient prices. Singh et al. [8] 
expounded  on a model  for dynamic  pricing  strategy,  tailored 
to Indian Railways, but generally applicable to restaurants. 
The authors' approach is underscored by the need for 
dynamic prices based on supply chain alterations. Popescu 
and Wu [9] worked on dynamic pricing with reference 
effects.  This should  work  well in restaurant  ingredient  price 
fluctuations without antagonizing the customers.  
 
Banerjee  and Gurung  [10] analyzed  whether  such models 
like ARIMA  and VAR  could  predict  the price  of vegetables, 
which is one of the basic needs for restaurants, which have 
fresh produce as a source. Namasudra et al. [11], as well as 
Deksnyte  and Lydeka  [12],  were  also eager  to make  the fact 
that dynamic model pricing for the alteration in  both supply 
chain and demand of consumers helps businesses incur 
profits without making prices burdening on the customer.  
 
It extends  these  concepts  by incorporating  ingredient  cost 
data coupled  with the real-time sales  and weather  patterns  that 
throw out a holistic pricing model. The project draws 
inspiration  from  Mishra  et al. [13] as well as Yamuna  et al. 
[14] and aims at the development  of a dynamic  framework  that 
tends  to adjust  the prices  based  on the interaction  between  the 
external  factors  such as weather  and the internal  drivers  such 
as ingredient costs.  
 
Literature work related to Machine  learning  for 
forecasting  sales  
Machine  learning  has become  an important  tool to foresee 
sales and make restaurant operations more efficient. 
Pavlyshenko [15] studied different models of machine 
learning for time series predictions. It was shown to make 
them quite efficient in sales trend forecasting using only 
historical information. Schmidt et al. [16] follows the same 
line of using  machine  learning  for restaurant  sales  forecasting. 
The study used neural networks for modeling, making it 
possible  to handle  complex  nonlinear  dependencies between 
various variables, such as weather, the prices of ingredients, 
and client preferences.  
 
 
In fact, Tsoumakas  [17] conducted a comprehensive 
review  of the approaches  machine  learning  applies  in terms  of 
predicting  food sales,  and indeed  finds  that ensemble  methods 
such as Random Forests are extremely effective even at 
surpassing conventional statistical models in handling vast, 
multi -dimensional datasets. Rane et al. [18], among others, 
also outlined how ML may look to enhance customer 
satisfaction through the prediction of their behavior while 
adjusting pricing accordingly.  
 
Other promising approaches include ARIMA models on 
time series forecasting, as suggested by Panda and Mohanty 
[19], and hybrid models, which combine machine learning 
techniques with statistical techniques, as suggested by 
Mahmoud  and Mohammed  [20].   
 
Hence,  these  models  can be exploited in the framework of Enhanced sales forecasting  in order to enhance the 
predictions toward the sales during seasonal variations in 
customer demand.  
III.METHODOLOGY  
Methodology  Steps:  
1. Data  Collection  
2. Data  Preprocessing  
3. Model  Selection  
4. Model  Training  and Evaluation  
5. Integration  for Real-Time  Analysis  
 
    DATA  COLLECTION  
First, the actual collection of detailed datasets that will be 
applied to the project will begin. Historical sales data will be 
used from the restaurant, showing the different categories such 
as highly perishable, perishable, non -perishable, and imported 
goods. Also, weather conditions, which involve temperature, 
humidity, and rainfall factors, will also be covered because 
external conditions play a big role in determining consumers' 
actions. This component shall collect ingredient price data and 
note the volatility from differences in circumstances or even the 
market drivers, potentially highlighting some supply -side-
related challenges. The primary sources will come from real -
time databases; governmental reports or publications will be 
used for good coverage of all factors. Here are the datasets 
employed for this project:  
- chennai_1990_2022_Madras.csv Source: Kaggle  
Historical weather data with climate parameters -temperature, 
about Chennai.  
-FAOSTAT_data_en_10 -05-2024*  
FAOSTAT dataset with ingredients, producer price series: in 
this analysis of forces driving the prices.  
Additional categorization done with the help of 
highly_perishable and non_perishable, perishable ,imported and 
price sensitivity.  
These shall be combined for integrated analyses between 
internal sales and outside influences in the environment as 
preparation to take effective decisions.  
 
    DATA  PREPROCESSING  
The preprocessing step is very intensive and begins post - 
gathering.  In this  process,  cleaning data  of all irregularities, 
or anomalies that are capable of deviating results, is 
followed by missing values being filled via either 
imputation or deletion. Here, features engineering take the 
lead whereby the proper attributes are defined or altered in 
such a way that they now do represent better relationships 
between variables that would be collected.  
For instance, classification of products as high perishable, 
perishable, non -perishable, and imported can enable the 
model to understand how each type of product reacts in 
different circumstances.  Then  the datasets  are brought  
together  into a single  integrated  dataset  with the sales  
records  correlated  to their respective weather and price 
records.  
   MODEL  SELECTION : 
Random  Forest  is selected considering  strength  for multiple 
input  features  with the feature  importance which, in  the case 
of this sensitivity and categorization analysis for the impact 
on sales,  might  be crucial.  ARIMA  was preferred  as it is very 
much applicable to the time series model thus allowing to Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 10,2026 at 15:43:45 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
understand seasonal patterns in ascending order of time in 
sales. These algorithms together provide a comprehensive 
approach to understanding and predicting sales patterns.  
    MODEL  TRAINING  AND  EVALUATION  
 
The next step is to train the selected models using the 
historical datasets. The data is typically split into training 
and testing  subsets,  allowing  the models  to learn  from  past 
sales patterns and evaluate their predictive accuracy. 
Hyperparameters are tuned for optimally maximizing 
performance, and the techniques of model validation used 
are cross -validation to ascertain that the model generalizes 
well to unseen data. Predictions' accuracy is checked by 
using  key performance  metrics,  namely  Mean  Squared  
Error (MSE). Lower the MSE, the more efficiently the 
model is able to capture the underlying relationships 
between variables in the data.  
    INTEGRATION  FOR  REAL-TIME ANALYSIS  
 
Finally,  the developed  models  are integrated  into a system 
that enables real -time demand and dynamic pricing.  
Integration facilitates continuous processing of new inputs, 
including real -time changes in weather and changes in 
ingredient prices. Once more data becomes available, these 
models can adjust their forecast in a matter of seconds, 
making it possible for restaurant owners to take the right 
inventory or pricing decisions. This is especially important 
for perishable products, as timely adjustment can help in 
preventing waste and optimize sales. The system also has 
specific  pricing  strategies  depending  on the type of 
product. Imported items and price -sensitive products are 
well taken care of.  
 
IV.EXPERIMENT AND ANALYSIS  
IMPLEMENTATION  OF DATA  VISUALIZATION  FOR 
INSIGHT EXTRACTION  
 
Density Plot Description:  
Comparing the actual and the fitted price distribution by the 
regression model. The actual prices are plotted in a filled KDE 
curve in blue colors, and the fitted ones in orange filled colors 
so they are differentiated by semi -transparent color fillings.  
 
 Figure 3 : Density Plot of Actual and Predicted Prices  
Figure 3  depicts the closeness of actual prices to the values that 
were predicted as model performance. Similar distributions 
represent the rightness of the model, and large deviations are representative of error areas in the model.  
 
Histogram Description:  
Plot histogram of error distribution of the regression model. The 
frequency of different error values is shown with 30 bins, and 
an overlay of KDE will smooth the error distribution.  
Figure 4 : Distribution of Predicted errors  
Figure 4 depicts  the extent to which model predictions can be 
accurate by bringing out both the frequency and dispersion of 
error. A distribution hugging closely around zero with relatively 
low dispersion would indicate higher accuracy while greater 
dispersion, or more significantly skewness suggests where 
adjustments in the models might need to be incorporated.   
 
Bar Plot Description : 
The above is a bar chart that represents the evaluation metrics 
for the Random Forest regression model, including MAE, MSE, 
and R² Score. All of them are in different colors so that it will 
be easier to compare.  
 
Figure 5 : Model Evaluation Metrics  
Figure 5 shows  a short visual summary of model performance. 
Lower MAE and MSE values relate to lesser errors, and a 
higher R² Score implies more effectiveness of the model in 
explaining variance. This evaluates the accuracy of the model 
and identifies where it needs improvement.  
 
Heatmap Description:  
A heat map of the price sensitivity level of various items. To 
the right is the color bar, which describes a range for the values 
on sensitivity.

Figure 8  line plot shows that for every product, the rate of

markers are given at each point for easy readability.  
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 10,2026 at 15:43:45 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
 
Figure 9 : Trends of Imported items over years on Non - Perishable 
Goods  
The figure  9 depicts the import curves, peak, and collapse for 
the non -perishable goods, thus affording a trend analysis to be 
made on the general products. Major volume shift over time 
can help the organization make appropriate adjustments with 
the inventory and supply chain, thus optimizing the non -
perishable stock's management.  
 
The line graph shows the pattern of the import volumes of the 
different types of non -perishable goods over the years. Each 
line that is a different color represents a different item and is 
denoted by a circular symbol to allow for easier visibility on 
the time axis. The change of lines over time is, from 2018 on, 
and very clearly shows a divergence among the products - 
some with marked increases in import volume and others had 
no increment and even a decreased volume. It is apparent there 
are diverging demand and supply of these products that also 
could involve restrained domestic production by consumer 
trends, international market preferences, trade agreements, or 
geopolitics.  
 
The parallels of the graph also show that the imports of non -
perishable imports were more similar in earlier years since 
they clustered in terms of volume, but by later years there is 
more of a spread of lengths, which illustrates that there were 
more common items of imports in earlier years, but, over time, 
has become more specialized by product types. This further 
emphasizes the development of tailored forecasting for the 
specific products instead of a general idea of demand for a 
category of items.  
 
The researchers looking at this will be able to see patterns in 
the trends over the years they can allow for planning to prepare 
for disruption, see opportunities for growth of products or 
other supply shortages or whether the stock of popular items 
has established ways to present to the agent, but without filling 
storage facilities with missing demand stock or incurring costs 
from supplying items that are not moving.  
 
To further validate the work, the architecture developed was 
tested with few real time data collected from 10 different 
restaurants  and our cafeteria, hostel mess.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
V.RESULT AND DISCUSSIONS  
 
TABLE 1 : 
 
 
Table shows the performance metrics of the sales predictive 
model developed through Mean Squared Error, and R -
squared  
For any model, different metrics such as MSE or R2 provide 
different insights into a model.  
 
MSE: This is an average squared difference between 
predictions and actual sales values. If the MSE is very low, it 
means the model is making predictions quite close to the 
observed value. The error is minimal. Results obtained in our 
study also reflected the fact that model values captured the 
trends related to the sales data, specifically those for 
perishable as well as highly perishable products, which are 
naturally sensitive to changes in such extraneous factors as 
weather.  
 
R-Squared (R²): This R² metric explains the variance in sales 
that could be contributed or explained by the model's inputs 
or inputs comprising its variables, such as weather variables 
and price sensitivity. The greater the value of R², the stronger 
the predictive strength of the model in capturing a factor that 
influences sales. From this analysis, it can be derived that R² 
varies category wise. Each category exhibits the sensitivity to 
the extent to which it reacts on variations in  
weather and change in price. For example, R² for more  
perishable products is rather higher, showing that there's a 
great aptness in the model for describing how the demand for 
it reacts to weather conditions changes.  
 
Overall, results of this study indicate predictability to be the 
highest for highly perishable items, followed by perishable, 
and lowest in the case of non -perishable. This is intuitive with 
the expectation that the product like fresh fruits would have a 
lot of responses due to environmental factors like temperature 
and seasonality, and products such as canned goods would 
have fewer fluctuations. This confirms the concept that real -
time pricing would help businesses maximise sales in sub -
product lines sensitive to particular circumstances.  
 
VI .CONCLUSION  
 
This project provides a solid, data -driven solution to the 
restaurant industry because restaurants can immediately 
respond to changes in weather and the prices of ingredients. 
The model maximizes the management of perishable, non -
perishable, and imported goods with the use of machine 
learning algorithms like Time Series Forecasting and 
Dynamic Pricing. Unlike static models applied earlier, this 
model will actually take real -time data, using predictive 
analytics to ensure the restaurants are responding well to the 
influence of extrinsic factors, but all of this without allowing 
it to compromise on the improvement in customer 
satisfaction, thus providing room for profit margins to be 
protected.  
 Metric  Value  
Mean Absolute Error (MAE)  5.31 
Mean Squared Error (MSE)  53.97  
R² Score  0.38

The restaurant managers, through the web of integrated 
interactive dashboards, establish consumer behavior and 
come up with fact -based decision -making about pricing as 
well as inventory levels. With the current weather dynamics, 
pricing and promotions will attract more customers, 
generating revenues. The entire project delivers a forward -
looking approach, equipping restaurants to handle an ever -
competitive market towards long term success and 
adaptability given the changing landscape of an industry.  
 
VII .FUTURE  ADVANCEMENT   
The project will look to the future to improve prediction 
accuracy by using ensemble learning and deep neural 
networks to identify complex patterns in sales and weather 
data.  Additionally,  Power  BI will be extended  for 
interactive visual dashboards to give deeper insights in 
real-time data - driven decisions. These developments can 
be of great help  to the restaurant industry in terms of 
optimizing ingredient inventory, dynamic pricing, and the 
sale strategy based on the actual demand forecast.

Abstract — Demand quantity determination plays a crucial 
role in operations management, particularly in impr oving 
purchasing decisions and stock management. This art icle 
considers the evaluation of daily demand forecastin g 
performance in a food and beverage SME using real d ata from 
sales trans- actions, focusing on the main ice crea m product. The 
methods used include a seven-day moving average (MA (7)) 
baseline model, a Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) mod el 
without optimization, and an LSTM optimized using a  grid 
search approach. Model performance is measured usin g RMSE 
(Root Mean Squared Error) and MAPE (Mean Absolute 
Percentage Error), but also through unit-based inve ntory 
simulations to assess operational impact. The study  showed that 
the optimized LSTM produced the best performance wi th an 
RMSE of 1.65 and a MAPE of 45.06%—better than MA(7)  with 
an RMSE of 1.84 and a MAPE of 46.60%. From an opera tional 
perspective, the best LSTM configuration was able t o 
completely eliminate lost demand, although it resul ted in an 
excess inventory of 1.57 units. 
Keywords— Time series forecasting, daily prediction,  
inventory optimization, LSTM, Grid Search, small me dium 
enterprise. 
I.  INTRODUCTION  
Demand forecasting plays a significant role in busi ness 
activities, serving as a basis for operational deci sions, 
including inventory management. For example, in the  Food 
and Beverage sector, products tend to spoil easily and 
experience fluctuating demand. Inaccurate forecasti ng can 
result in losses: insufficient inventory leads to l ost sales 
opportunities, while excess storage increases the r isk of 
spoilage. 
Efficiency in inventory management plays a crucial role in 
establishing a source of competitive advantage for businesses; 
companies that are able to more accurately align st ock levels 
with demand patterns generally operate more effecti vely and 
efficiently [1]. In the context of small and medium - sized 
enterprises (SMEs), the ability to reliably forecas t demand is 
key to survival and competition in an increasingly competitive 
marketplace. 
Most small businesses (SMEs) often base their purch asing 
and inventory procurement decisions on intuition an d the 
owner’s experience [2] . This approach carries the potential 
for significant losses, particularly when demand pa tterns 
experience unexpected changes or fluctuations. This  
demonstrates the need for a more systematic, data-d riven 
approach to demand forecasting to support operation al 
decision-making in SMEs. This research utilized a real sales transaction dat aset 
obtained from a micro and small enterprise (SME) op erating 
in the food and beverage sector. The primary produc t offered 
by this SME is ice cream, but they also offer a var iety of 
complementary food and beverage menu items. The 
transaction data used represents actual daily sales  activity, 
thus reflecting the business’s actual operational c onditions. 
Therefore, this study examines several time series- based 
forecasting approaches through a comparative study.  As a 
baseline method, a seven-day moving average (MA(7))  model 
is applied to represent simple forecasting practice s. Next, a 
Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) model is tested as a deep 
learning approach to capture more complex temporal patterns 
in daily sales data. Furthermore, this study also e xamines the 
hyperparameter optimization process in the LSTM mod el to 
improve forecasting performance. 
II.  RELATED WORK  
In the context of operations management, it is impo rtant to 
consider the relationship between inventory decisio ns and the 
quality of projected demand information. Forecastin g acts as 
an initial mechanism that transforms historical dat a into 
estimates of future product needs, so that procurem ent can be 
managed more systematically and in a controlled man ner [3], 
[4]. Furthermore, these projections are used as one  of the main 
sources in operational decision making, for example  in 
determining order quantities, setting rescheduling,  and 
adjusting inventory levels to stay in line with act ual demand 
patterns [5]. 
The moving average method is a basic forecasting 
approach that builds predictions based on the avera ge demand 
value over several previous periods. The algorithm’ s 
practicality and simplicity make this method often chosen by 
practitioners due to its ease of implementation and  low 
computational requirements. In addition to demand 
forecasting, moving averages are also popular in te chnical 
analysis of financial markets due to their relevanc e in 
identifying price trends and supporting trading dec ision-
making through analysis of historical price movemen t 
patterns. [6]. 
According to Ananth et al. [7] research, simple for ecasting 
approaches remain relevant for sales prediction bec ause they 
found that by using Simple Moving Average and Simpl e 
Linear Regression, historical average-based estimat es are able 
to provide competitive levels of accuracy without h aving to 
rely on more complex models. 2026 International Conference on Current Research in Artificial Intelligence and Data Science (ICCRAIDS) | 979-8-3315-6808-5/26/$31.00 ©2026 IEEE | DOI: 10.1109/ICCRAIDS67816.2026.11519654
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:29:43 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
Unlike simple statistical approaches, Long Short-Te rm 
Memory (LSTM) was developed as a specialized varian t of 
Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) to handle long-term 
dependencies in sequential data. By integrating mem ory cells 
con- trolled by a gate mechanism, namely input gate s, forget 
gates, and output gates, this model is able to regu late the flow 
of information across time. This mechanism allows t he 
network to determine for itself which information t o retain, 
ignore, or pass on to the next period. This capabil ity makes 
LSTM very effective in modeling complex temporal 
dynamics, including time series forecasting problem s. [8], [9]. 
In the context of automotive pricing, Long et al. ( 2023) 
proposed a hybrid GM(1,1)-LSTM approach and compare d 
the results; their study concluded that the combina tion of 
traditional pre- diction methods and LSTM tends to improve 
forecasting accuracy while maintaining sensitivity to external 
economic fluctuations. In the retail sec- tor, Kaun chi et al. 
(2021) applied a CNN–LSTM architecture to grocery s ales 
data and reported high accuracy, demonstrating that  deep 
learning can effectively extract complex temporal p atterns 
from large-scale sales data. In e-commerce sales fo recasting, 
Yan (2023) studied the application of CNN–LSTM and 
concluded that the approach yielded better performa nce than 
general neural networks. 
In developing machine learning models, hyperparamet er 
selection is a crucial step that directly impacts t he model's 
performance, generalizability, and computational ef ficiency. 
The hyperparameter optimization process aims to fin d the 
most appropriate combination of setting parameters so that the 
model can operate optimally on the data used [10]. 
Hyperparameter optimization is a crucial factor in 
improving the performance of machine learning model s, 
including neural network-based algorithms such as L STM. 
Siregar et al. demonstrated that a combination of g rid search 
and random search can improve the accuracy of class ification 
models by reducing reliance on manual parameter 
determination [11]. In the context of time series f orecasting, 
Buslim et al. demonstrated that the performance of RNN, 
GRU, and LSTM is significantly influenced by 
hyperparameter optimization strategies, with the be st results 
obtained through the application of grid search [12 ]. Ibrahim 
then proposed Improved Grid Search for LSTM, which 
resulted in significant accuracy improvements in hi gh-
resolution electricity price forecasting [13]. 
III.  METHODOLOGY  
This study uses the Point of Sale (POS) system of a  micro 
and small enterprise (SME) in the food and beverage  sector as 
a data source. Its operational pattern is similar t o the business 
model of an established franchised ice cream outlet . The data 
set was collected continuously from March 2023 to t he end of 
December 2025, thus covering a fairly long medium-t erm 
sales observation period. The choice of daily data resolution 
aligns with the management practices of small busin ess 
owners in planning raw material purchases and inven tory 
control. 
The analysis focuses on a single product, vanilla i ce 
cream, which serves as the core product and exhibit s a 
consistent sales pattern since the business began. This ice 
cream was selected due to its complete data and rel atively 
stable sales pattern, suitable for use in time seri es analysis. 
After calendar alignment and handling of non-transa ction days, the final dataset used in this study consiste d of 
approximately 1,030 daily observation points. 
In this study, the forecasting objective is vanilla  ice cream 
sales, represented by monthly aggregation, as shown  in Figure 
1. In the early stages of business operations, sale s fluctuations 
appeared relatively high, particularly in mid-2023.  Over time, 
sales patterns showed a more controlled tendency, a lthough 
seasonal variations were mild and changes in direct ion 
between periods still occurred sporadically. 
 
Fig. 1.  Vanilla Ice Cream Monthly Sales Trends 
Prior to developing the forecasting model, daily sa les data 
were analyzed exploratively to identify the basic 
characteristics of the time series. Initial analysi s included 
seasonal decomposition to separate trend, seasonal,  and 
residual components as a basis for understanding th e data 
structure. Next, patterns of temporal dependence be tween 
observations were examined using the autocorrelatio n 
function (ACF) and partial autocorrelation function  (PACF). 
Furthermore, the distribution of occurrences of non -
transaction days and transaction days was analyzed to capture 
the operational implications inherent in small busi ness daily 
sales data. 
Based on the initial analysis, the dataset was sepa rated into 
training and testing data. All observations from Ma rch 2023 
to December 2025 were used as the training baseline  for 
model development, while the testing data was limit ed to the 
period from January 1–7, 2026, to evaluate very sho rt-term 
forecasting performance. The seven- day evaluation horizon 
was intentionally selected to reflect the weekly pr ocurement 
cycle practiced by the small food and beverage SMEs . 
Testing was conducted on the compiled training data , 
using a seven-day moving average (MA(7)) as the bas eline 
model that predicts values based on the average his torical 
sales within a weekly cycle. This seven-day window was 
chosen based on the common practice of small busine sses in 
procuring raw materials weekly. Then, in the next s tage, a 
Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) model was applied as a 
deep learning approach to capture temporal patterns  in the 
daily sales data. 
Model performance is measured using two 
complementary approaches: the first is statisticall y based, 
using RMSE and MAPE to measure accuracy, while the 
second focuses on the operational context by analyz ing a 
weekly purchasing cycle simulation based on forecas ting 
results. In the simulation, the number of units ass umed to be 
purchased is calculated as the accumulated daily de mand 
predictions over the test period, providing a pract ical 
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:29:43 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
understanding of the model’s impact on inventory de cisions. 
The overall research work-flow, including data prep aration, 
forecasting modelling, and operational evaluation, is 
summarized in Fig. 2. 
 
Fig. 2.  Methodology flow of forecasting and inventory evalu ation 
In this study, operational evaluation is con- ducte d through 
several inventory performance indicators. The term “unit” 
refers to a single serving of vanilla ice cream sol d by the SME. 
Forecasting models produce continuous numerical pre dictions 
representing expected demand levels, which may resu lt in 
fractional values. These values represent statistic al estimates 
rather than actual physical quantities, and in prac tical 
inventory decisions they would typically be rounded  to whole 
units. 
Initial purchase units are defined as the total uni ts ordered 
at the beginning of the period, calculated from the  daily 
demand forecast. Actual demand during the test peri od is 
indicated by total demand units. The difference bet ween the 
purchase amount and actual demand gives rise to two  
conditions: overstock units, which reflect the rema ining stock 
at the end of the period after demand has been full y met, and 
lost demand units, which indicate unmet demand due to 
limited inventory. To provide a concise measure of 
procurement decision accuracy, the purchase–demand gap 
indicator is also used, which is calculated as the difference 
between the total units purchased and the total act ual demand 
within one operational cycle. 
IV.  RESULT AND DISCUSSION  
Figure 3 shows the results of seasonal decompositio n on 
daily sales data, and it appears that the trend, se asonal, and 
residual components have been clearly separated. In  the early stages of business activity, the trend indicates si gnificant 
changes in sales levels before adopting a more cons istent 
pattern in subsequent periods. The identified seaso nal pattern 
is repetitive and tends to be consistent, indicatin g a demand 
cycle that ranges on a weekly scale for daily data.  Meanwhile, 
the residual component represents short-term sales variations 
that are not fully captured by trends or seasonalit y, and is a 
common characteristic often seen in small businesse s where 
demand uncertainty tends to be high. 
Fig. 3.  Seasonal Decomposition of daily sales 
The mapping of temporal dependencies in daily sales  data 
was analyzed using the autocorrelation function (AC F) and 
partial autocorrelation function (PACF), shown in F igures 4 
and 5, respectively. The ACF analysis reveals a pos itive 
relationship at several initial lags, indicating th at sales values 
in the previous period still contribute to sales mo vements in 
the current period. Meanwhile, for the PACF, the di rect 
influence tends to decrease as the lag increases, s o that the 
interperiod relationship tends to be limited to onl y a few initial 
time intervals. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Fig. 4.  Daily sales autocorrelation 
 
Fig. 5.  Partial autocorrelation of daily sales 
Figure 6 depicts the distribution pattern be- tween  days 
involving sales transactions and days without trans actions. 
This visualization shows that although sales activi ty occurs on 
most of the observed days, the proportion of days w ithout 
transactions remains quite dominant. This condition  reflects 
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:29:43 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
the discontinuity of daily transactions, which need s to be 
considered in sales forecasting modeling for small businesses. 
 
Fig. 6.  Comparison of days with zero and non-zero sales 
In general, the results of diagnostic and explorato ry 
analysis indicate that the sales time series is inf luenced by 
weekly seasonal patterns, short-term temporal relat ionships, 
and relatively high levels of daily variation. For forecasting, 
two models are used, namely MA(7) and LSTM. To buil d the 
LSTM model, a single LSTM layer with 32 units and a  30-day 
input window is used, MinMax normalization is perfo rmed, 
the use of the Adam optimizer, a mean squared error  loss 
function, a batch size of 16, and an early stopping  mechanism 
during training. 
From Table I, it can be seen that LSTM shows better  
statistical performance than MA(7), because the RMS E and 
MAPE values in LSTM are lower.

In contrast, from an operational perspective based on 
inventory simulation, MA(7) produces slightly lower  total 
inefficiency than LSTM. This finding indicates that  increasing 
forecasting accuracy is not always directly proport ional to 
improving operational performance, so model evaluat ion in 
the SME context needs to consider both statistical aspects and 
inventory implications simultaneously. 
In the context of perishable products such as ice c ream, 
excess inventory may lead to spoilage costs if the product 
cannot be sold within its shelf life. Conversely, l ost demand 
represents missed sales opportunities and potential  revenue 
loss. Therefore, inventory decisions involve a trad e-off 
between the risk of spoilage due to overstock and t he financial 
impact of lost sales caused by insufficient invento ry. 
Hyperparameter optimization in the LSTM model was 
performed using grid search by constructing a searc h space 
that includes four main hyperparameters: (1) window , tested 
at values of 7, 14, and 30; (2) number of units, te sted at 16, 32, 
and 64; (3) number of layers, tested at 1 and 2 lay ers; and (4) 
dropout, tested at 0.0 and 0.2. For each combinatio n, the data was normalized using MinMax scaling based on the tr aining 
data, then the model was trained using the Adam opt imizer 
and the MSE loss function with a batch size of 16 a nd a 
maximum of 60 epochs. Predictions for the test peri od of 
January 1-7, 2026 were then evaluated using RMSE an d 
MAPE, and operationally evaluated through unit-base d 
inventory simulations. The best configuration was s elected 
based on the priority order of minimizing lost dema nd units 
first, then minimizing RMSE as a statistical criter ion. 
TABLE II.  COMPARISON OF THE BEST PERFORMANCE OF MA(7)  AND 
LTSM
