 Challenges in Milk Yield Forecasting and Feed 
Optimization: A Comparative Study of AI -based 
and Quantum -enhanced Dairy Systems  
Renu Chaudhary  
Department of Computer Science and Engineering  
M.M. Engineering College  
Maharishi Markandeshwar  (Deemed to be University)   
Ambala, Haryana, India  
mail.renuchaudhary@gmail.com  Rajeev Gupta  
Department of Computer Science and Engineering,  
M.M. Engineering College  
Maharishi Markandeshwar (Deemed to b e University)  
Ambala, Haryana, India  
rajeev.gupta@mmumullana.org   
 
 
Abstrac t—The global dairy industry  is under increasing 
pressure to enhance milk production forecasting and milk feed 
optimization in the face of increasing operational costs and 
sustainability demands . Lots of classical AI -based mechanisms 
have been suggested by researchers, but s till, there  is no 
systematic comparison of classical AI  and quantum -enhanced 
methods of handling such issues. The purpose of t he study is to 
compare classical AI and quantum -enhanced methods for milk 
production  forecasting and milk feed optimization, with 
comparative advantages, weaknesses, and implementation . 
With concern of m ethodology  used, a  multi -dimensional 
comparative analysis of the classical machine learning and 
quantum computing methods was performed with the 
examination of the following dimensions of their computational 
efficiency, accuracy of predic tions, scaling, optimization 
capacity, and feasibility of practical implementation in dairy 
farming situations. The major findings of this study are that 
classical machine learning algorithms obtain prediction rates 
of 80 -85% and are operationally mature s olutions ; they are 
poor at processing high -dimensional data and performing 
multi -objective optimization in real -time. The feed costs , which  
are 60 -70% of the total operational costs , are controlled using 
the fixed optimization measures that are not dynamic . 
Quantum computing solutions are based on superposition and 
entanglement to provide theoretically superior performance in 
optimization, but the performance gains obtained by 
benchmarking  are not proven to be effective in the dairy 
environment, and signifi cant challenges in hardware 
availability, system integration, and deployment preparedness 
mean that it cannot be adopted in agriculture in the near 
future. With concern of i nterpretations and implications , it can 
be seen that there is a striking contrast b etween the classical AI 
systems, which can be deployed and scaled on the current 
infrastructure, and quantum -enhanced systems, which can be 
applied in complex optimization problems through 
transformational response but must be confined to 
experimental sett ings until hardware maturity.  The 
recommendation to stakeholders in the dairy industry is to use 
classical AI solutions in the short run and strategically observe 
the quantum advancements in the future to incorporate it. This 
structure promotes accurate  livestock management and 
provides a guide towards sustainable production systems of 
dairy with the capacity to fulfill the projected global food 
security requirements.  
Keywords —Milk Yield Prediction, Feed Optimization, 
Quantum Machine Learning, Pr ecision Dairy Farming, 
Agricultural Decision Support, Computational Optimization  
I. INTRODUCTION  
Dairy farming represents a fundamental component of 
global agricultural systems, directly contributing to food security for populations projected to reach 9.73 billion by the 
year 2050 [1]. The contemporary dairy sector is subjected to 
multidimensional constraints that necessitate concomitant 
optimization of a variety of conflicting goals such as 
productivity improvement, cost minimization, environmental 
sustainabi lity, and animal welfare preservation [1]. The most 
important performance measure is milk production, which is 
affected by a great number of interdependent factors , 
including genetic predisposition, nutritional consumption, 
management, environmental factor s, and the health condition 
of animals [2]. Modern dairy enterprises produce 
heterogeneous data flows as a result of automated milking 
systems, environmental sensors, and precision feeding 
devices , and bring opportunities and analytical problems to 
the far m management decision -making  [3]. 
A. Problem Background and Motivation  
Feed optimization represents approximately 60 –70% of 
total dairy farm operational costs [4], [21].  Optimal nutrition 
is achieved through strict balancing of sources of energy, 
protein, min erals , and vitamins , depending on specific 
animal factors such as lactation stage, body weight , and 
health status [5]. Technologies of precision feeding that 
include sensors and artificial intelligence are a better 
improvement compared to conventional ones , because they 
allow tracking intake in real -time and adjusting diets 
automatically [3]. The biological complexity of the processes 
of milk production makes optimization problems that cannot 
be addressed by traditional computational methods [4].  The 
dairy management systems are now dominated by classical 
artificial intelligence methods, more especially the machine 
learning algorithms, which exhibit fair accuracy in milk yield 
prediction and optimization of operations [8]. According to a 
study by Zhang et al ., neural networks that include 
environmental factors, feeding information, and 
physiological measurements are able to predict with 
accuracies of 80-85% [9]. Liseune et al. established that deep 
learning models that are trained on thirty days of historical  
data can produce correlation coefficients of 0.77 in lactation 
curve prediction [10]. In spite of these successes, classical 
approaches are faced with computational problems when 
handling high -dimensional data, nonlinear biological 
interactions , and real -time multi -objective optimization [11].  
B. Research Gap and Problem Statement  
The computational challenge manifests across multiple 
dimensions in contemporary dairy farming systems [12]. The 
high-dimensional feature spaces with many interacting 
variables require large processing resources that are a burden 
on the classical computing  architecture [13]. Multifaceted 
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:37 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
biological cross -functionalities between the input parameters 
and production outputs are not easy to model accurately 
using conventional algorithm methods [14]. Temporal 
dependencies further complicate the problem , and most  
classical approaches cannot model them with enough 
accuracy [14]. The nature of real -time optimization needs 
requires dynamism in adapting to changing environmental 
conditions, behaviors of animals and variable resource 
availability, which are beyond the responsiveness of the 
classical models , which are static [15].  
Quantum computing has been a potentially revolutionary 
paradigm that takes advantage of the concepts of 
superposition and entanglement to search a large solution 
space by using parallel process ing techniques [16]. Recent 
quantum machine learning research shows promising signs, 
with quantum models getting a 97.01% accuracy in 
comparison to 96.5% of classical approaches in 
neuroinformatics applications [17]. Peral -Garcia et al. have 
noted that Qua ntum Neural Networks can retain 75% of 
accuracy even when the system noise is 40%  of the system, 
whereas Quantum -Cognitive Neural Networks can train at a 
rate of fifty times more than classical counterparts [18]. 
Quantum algorithms such as the Quantum Appr oximate 
Optimization Algorithm and the Variational Quantum 
Eigensolvers provide new ways of solving combinatorial 
optimization problems , which are hard to solve using 
classical algorithms [19].  
Although both computational paradigms have improved 
over time, there still exist  a lot of knowledge gaps in 
comparative knowledge about implementation issues [20]. 
Classical artificial intelligence literature focuses on 
capabilities and gives limited consi deration to the 
computational constraints and scalability limits [21]. The 
study of quantum computing is largely theoretical and 
without a critical examination of barriers to practice and 
complexities of system integration [19]. Such a lack of 
knowledge ma kes it uncertain to the stakeholders who are 
involved in making technology adoption decisions in 
agricultural situations [6].  
C. Research Objectives  
This study performs a systematic comparative study of 
the issues in predicting milk output and feed support in  
classical artificial intelligence -based and quantum -enhanced 
dairy production models. The targeted objectives are:  
• Determining and classifying the problems of 
computational challenges in both paradigms using a 
systematic literature review.  
• Comparison of c omparative performance 
potentialities such as accuracy, processing efficiency , 
and scalability characteristics.  
• Evaluating the ability to implement based on 
hardware needs, the maturity of the software , and the 
complexity of implementation.  
• Delivering evid ence -based suggestions to the 
stakeholders in selecting technologies in precision 
livestock management settings.  
The research study belongs to the expanding body of 
knowledge on precision agriculture since it offers 
systematized  knowledge on relative merit s, limitations, and 
implementation problems in both forms of calculation  [7]. D. Key Terminology  
Precision Dairy Farming is a management philosophy 
that involves the use of information technology and thorough 
data analysis to optimize the productivity, econom ic 
efficiency, environmental sustainability , and animal welfare 
in dairy operations [6]. The quantum Superposition is a 
quantum mechanical phenomenon in which quantum bits are 
present in many states at once until they are measured, which 
allows them to explore multiple solution spaces in parallel 
[16]. Quantum Entanglement is a quantum correlation of the 
particles  where the state of one particle instantly affects 
another despite the spatial distance, and it can perform 
complex computation tasks that classical computers are not 
able to perform [18]. V ariational Quantum Circuits are 
parameterized quantum algorithms, which are optimized via 
hybrid training algorithms that combine classical and 
quantum training methods in machine learning tasks [19].  
II. REVIEW  OF LITERATURE  
This part is a review of existing literature on 
optimization of dairy farming and computational methods, 
which summarizes the results of the classical AI and 
quantum computing literature to provide the theoretical 
framework of the comparative analysis made in this paper.  
A. Dairy Farming Opti mization Requirements  
Modern dairy farming optimization encompasses 
multidimensional objectives requiring sophisticated 
analytical methodologies for effective integration [3]. 
Halachmi et al. highlighted that the purpose of production 
optimization is to re ach the maximum possible milk 
production without considering the quality, through proper 
genetic selection, proper nutrition control, and a regulated 
environment [3]. Resource management is related to feed 
formulation, water consumption, and energy consump tion 
with the aim of reducing the cost and satisfying the 
physiological requirements of individual animals [4]. 
According to Gonzalez -Rivas et al., these features of 
environmental sustainability are the reduction of greenhouse 
gas emissions, waste manageme nt efficiency, and land use 
optimization [7]. The animal welfare parameters include 
regular health examinations, stress relievers, and behavioral 
control systems [6]. These objectives are usually mutually 
exclusive , and multi -objective optimization systems  are 
required , which cannot be done by traditional single -
objective operational paradigms [5].  
The dairy farming systems are complex in nature due to 
the dynamic interactions of the management practices, the 
environmental factors , and the biological proces ses [7]. The 
genetically determined basic production capacity and 
disease -resistant  traits are genetically determined [2]. The 
nutritional inputs directly influence the quantity of milk and 
the quality of composition  through complex metabolism [4]. 
Quality  of housing, ambient temperatures, and the level of 
humidity are some of the environmental factors that have a 
massive impact on the results of comfort and productivity of 
animals [7]. The overall performance of the operations is 
affected by the management  practices, such as milking 
schedule, health interventions, and breeding choices [21]. 
These features imply nonlinear interactions that create 
optimization landscapes that are inaccessible to conventional 
analysis plans [26].  
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:37 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
B. Classical AI Applications in D airy Management  
Classical artificial intelligence applications in dairy 
farming employ diverse methodologies for prediction and 
optimization tasks [8]. Dongre and Gandhi showed that 
neural networks are one of the key tools of milk yield 
prediction, where m ulti-layer perceptrons  and recurrent 
architectures  are used to model time -dependent relationships 
[8]. Support vector machines are used in classification , such 
as disease detection and breeding selection [20]. 
Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average model s and 
exponential smoothing methods are time series analysis 
techniques that deal with the seasonal patterns of production 
[13]. Random forests and decision trees offer interpretable 
management decision support features [8].  
Milk yield prediction performan ce using machine 
learning varies substantially based on methodological 
approach and dataset characteristics [9]. According to Zhang 
et al., neural network techniques that use environmental 
factors, feeding information, and physiological indicators as 
input s yield accuracies of between 80-85%  [9]. Correlation 
coefficients of up to 0.77 were reported by Liseune et al. for 
the prediction of lactation curves using deep learning in 
controlled experimental studies, and these are quoted here 
merely as evidence fro m previous literature, and not as 
results of the current study. [10]. Murphy et al. discovered 
that simpler statistical models can be more effective than 
complex machine learning algorithms when the quality or 
quantity of data is inadequate, which implies that the 
methodology should be matched to the data properties [13]. 
These performance levels are realized when the 
computational limits are imposed to limit the real -time 
processing capabilities and scalability to large -scale 
operations [12].  
In classical feed optimization techniques, linear 
programming models are mostly used, which assume that 
nutritional needs are fixed and ingredient prices are fixed [4]. 
Recently, Usigbe et al. offered numerous ideas of many -
objective optimization framework s of dairy cattle feed 
development that address a variety of conflicting objectives 
at the same time, which is a step forward compared to the 
conventional single -objective models [5]. Akintan et al. have 
highlighted the need to establish a direct relations hip 
between feed formulation and milk quantity, quality, and 
animal health by using data -driven decision -making 
processes [4]. These methods do not have mechanisms of 
dynamic adaptation to the changing circumstances, and 
reinforcement learning applications  are still not widespread 
in livestock settings [22].  
C. Critical Analysis of Existing Studies  
The existing literature on milk production forecasting 
mainly focuses on accuracy in predictions through traditional 
machine learning techniques, without much emphasis on 
computational scalability and real -time optimization issues in 
a large -scale dairy se tting [9], [20]. Despite the promising 
outcomes of deep learning methods in predictive 
performance, the majority of the studies are carried out on 
controlled data sets and do not take into account the real -life 
limitations , such as the dynamic regulation o f the feed rate 
and the fusion of multi -sensor  data. Theoretical 
investigations on the applications of quantum computing in 
agriculture are rather vivid, and the experimental validation 
has been done on typical optimization problems and not on 
dairy farm d atasets [17], [18]. Accordingly, no direct comparative insight is available into the viability of 
implementation, cost effectiveness , and deployment 
preparedness of the two paradigms. This study paper 
addresses this gap by providing a comparative study of the 
two paradigms with regard to computational efficiency, 
scalability and implementation feasibility. Recent research 
published in the International Journal of Information 
Technology has experimented with the use of machine 
learning and data -driven method s in the context of smart 
agriculture and dairy farms , with special interest in milk 
yield prediction, resource optimization, and decision support 
systems  [32]–[36]. 
III. METHODOLOGY  
The research uses a comparative systematic approach to 
assess classical AI and  quantum -enhanced solutions in 
various dimensions of analysis. The subsections below 
summarize the research framework, data sources , and 
evaluation criteria to be employed to organize the 
comparative analysis.  
A. Research Framework  
This study employs a system atic comparative analysis 
framework to evaluate challenges in milk yield forecasting 
and feed optimization under both artificial intelligence -based 
and quantum -enhanced dairy systems [2]. The research 
design incorporates three fundamental elements that inc lude 
literature synthesis to provide overall knowledge integration, 
theoretical analysis to provide conceptual insight, and 
comparative evaluation to provide performance evaluation 
[29]. These combined approaches allow us to determine the 
computational con straints, evaluate the technological 
capabilities , and check the possibility of implementation in 
either of the two computational paradigms [30]. The 
framework offers a systematic methodology in the resolution 
of research goals via multi -dimensional analys is that 
involves computational performance, scalability attributes, 
implementation practicability, and application 
appropriateness aspects [2].  
B. Literature Collection and Selection  
Literature collection proceeded through systematic 
database searches across multiple scholarly repositories , 
including Web of Science, Institute of Electrical and 
Electronics Engineers Xplore, ScienceDirect, and specialized 
agricultural journals [29]. The search strategies were based 
on the combination of specific keywords such as  quantum 
machine learning, da iry farming optimization, precision 
livestock management, milk yield prediction, feed 
optimization, and agricultural decision support systems [30]. 
The temporal selection criteria were based on peer-reviewed  
articles of the las t five years to make sure that the 
technological advances were up -to-date and seminal articles 
defining the basic concepts were included , irrespective of the 
year of publication [29].  
Inclusion criteria were direct relevance to one or more 
areas of focus: agricultural optimization methodologies, 
dairy farming applications, quantum computing 
implementations, or classical machine learning applications 
to livestock management situations [30]. The exclusion 
criteria were used to remove studies that were not pee r 
reviewed, studies that were solely interested in non -dairy 
agricultural applications without transferable insights , and 
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:37 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
studies that only provided preliminary conference abstracts 
without a detailed methodology [30 ]. 
C. Evaluation Criteria  
The assessment framework establishes systematic 
parameters for comparing artificial intelligence -based and 
quantum -enhanced solutions across multiple evaluative 
dimensions [14]. The performance measures include 
accuracy of prediction based on standard stat istical measures, 
computational efficiency based on processing time 
requirements, operational speed based on the rate of 
algorithm execution, and optimization quality based on the 
measures of solution optimality [9]. Scalability testing is the 
test of degr adation of system performance or maintenance 
with increasing data volumes, increasing farm sizes , and 
increasing problem complexity [15].  Some of the challenges 
in computational complexity include processing capacity 
limitations, algorithmic complexity lim its, and demands on 
computational resources [11]. The challenges associated with 
data relate to dimensionality issues of features, data quality 
needs, data temporal consistency needs, and heterogeneous 
data integration challenges [12]. The implementation i ssues 
include the limitation of hardware accessibility, the level of 
maturity of software development, the availability of 
technical expertise, and the cost considerations [15]. This is a 
systematic assessment scheme that allows objective 
comparison and pr ofiles of paradigm -specific constraints 
[24].  
IV. CHALLENGES IN AI-BASED DAIRY SYSTEMS  
Both classical artificial intelligence -based and quantum -
enhanced approaches encounter distinct challenge profiles 
influencing practical deployment in dairy farming contexts  
[24].  
 
 
Fig. 1.  Challenge Classification and Severity Mapping Comparative 
Analysis of Implementation Barriers  These issues are visually compared in detail in Figure 1, 
which plots the degree of complexity versus the degree of 
deployment effect in both paradigm s. The bubble 
visualization shows that the classical systems have mostly 
implementation and optimization limitations, whereas 
quantum systems have fundamental accessibility and 
technical limitations.  
A. Milk Yield Forecasting Limitations  
Classical machine lea rning methods demonstrate inherent 
limitations in modeling biological system complexity 
associated with milk production [9 ]. Zhang et al. reported 
that neural networks reach accuracy plateaus at 80-85% 
which cannot be significantly increased by more model 
complexity and training data volumes [9]. The nonlinear 
interactions between genetic, nutritional, environmental, and 
physiological variables produce feature spaces, which the 
classical algorithms find difficult to model appropriately 
[14].  
The issue of fe ature engineering is still a challenge to the 
milk yield prediction system [13]. The process of selecting 
and transforming input variables is of great importance to 
model effectiveness , but highly relies on domain knowledge 
and trial and error [27]. Stream s of sensor data create a high-
dimensional feature space that is  computationally complex 
and prone to overfitting [12]. Temporal dependency 
modeling is required by sequential measurements, which 
necessitates a more complex implementation in the form of 
recurrent neural networks [13].  
The real -world forecasting accuracy is constrained by the 
data quality and availability [27]. Sensor failures introduce 
gaps in training data that compromise the reliability of the 
model [15]. Environmental interference causes measurement 
noise that propagates prediction pipeline errors [14]. New 
animals have no historical records, which makes it 
impossible to make precise individual predictions in the early 
stages of lactation when the forecast is of the greatest 
importance [27 ]. 
B. Feed Optimization Constraints  
Classical feed optimization employs linear programming , 
assuming constant nutritional requirements and ingredient 
costs [4]. Such assumptions are poor in dynamic dairy 
farming , where animal requirements vary with lactation 
stages and seasonal changes in environmental conditions [5]. 
The non -linearity in the associations between nutrient 
compositions and milk production results cannot be 
represented in linear models [4]. The appr oach of single -
objective optimization that does not take into account 
conflicting priorities , such as animal welfare and 
environmental sustainability , is not enough [22].  
Live optimization is a drastic drawback of the 
conventional feed optimization systems  [5]. Traditional 
solutions calculate best formulations at specific time 
intervals instead of continuously adapting to changing 
conditions [4]. The temperature variations in the 
environment affect the energy needs of animals, but the set 
formulas are not a ble to react in time [7]. The variability of 
individual animals in digestive efficiency implies that 
individual feeding programs would be more successful than 
classical optimization models [5].  
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:37 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
C. System Integration Complexities  
Contemporary dairy operations generate heterogeneous 
data from automated milking systems, environmental 
sensors, feeding equipment, and health monitoring devices 
[3]. There are technical challenges of integrating these 
divergent streams of data [28]. The s ensors utilize various 
data formats that demand a lot of preprocessing [29]. The 
difference in frequency of measurements between devices 
makes synchronization difficult [3].  The deployment of 
artificial intelligence -based dairy systems is limited by the 
requirements of the computational infrastructure [28]. Deep 
learning architectures require large processing resources 
when training and performing inference tasks [20]. The 
locations of farms usually do not have sufficient computing 
facilities to do local op timization [22]. Cloud computing 
brings the issue of latency that cannot be used in decision -
making that is time -sensitive  [28].  The system of agricultural 
artificial intelligence software is still in its infancy relative 
to other application sectors [29].  Development of ready -
made models specially developed to suit dairy farming is 
needed [30]. Model training and benchmarking . 
Standardized datasets are still scarce [20]. Integration with 
the existing farm management systems requires custom 
interface develo pment [29].  
D. Decision -Making and Operational Challenges  
Decision support systems that are based on artificial 
intelligence often work as black boxes that give 
recommendations without any clear explanation [8]. This 
opaqueness creates mistrust in dairy farme rs who would like 
to know the basis of management decisions [21]. Farming 
scenarios require transparency since any wrong decision will 
have delayed but drastic effects that will be felt in the long 
term [8].  Exposure to new circumstances exposes intrinsic 
weaknesses in current methods of artificial intelligence [11]. 
Machine learning models that are trained with historical data 
do not perform in the situation that is not within training 
distributions [14]. Climate change presents new 
environmental trends  that have never been experienced 
before [7]. Transfer learning techniques raise the complexity 
of the processes and require large amounts of new data [11].  
Precision dairy systems that are based on artificial 
intelligence require hardware installation and ma intenance 
overhead  [15]. Sensor networks must be placed well, 
calibrated , and replaced periodically [3]. Failure of the 
equipment interferes with data collection and interrupts the 
work of the systems [15].  
V. CHALLENGES IN QUANTUM -ENHANCED DAIRY SYSTEMS  
Even  though quantum -enhanced systems have a high 
theoretical potential, they have significant obstacles that 
restrict their use in agricultural settings. In this section, the 
author will analyze the key challenges in terms of theoretical, 
hardware, and integra tion aspects that limit the use of 
quantum computing in managing dairy farms at the moment.  
A. Theoretical and Conceptual Barriers  
Quantum computing concepts , including superposition 
and entanglement , represent fundamentally different 
paradigms from classical computation, creating substantial 
conceptual barriers for agricultural researchers [16]. Dairy 
optimization problems need to be converted into quantum -compatible formulations, which necessitate th e collaboration 
of interdisciplinary skills in quantum physics, computer 
science, and agricultural biology, a skill set that is difficult to 
find in research teams [18]. The choice of algorithm to use in 
particular dairy applications is not well understood , since the 
selection of the best quantum methods needs a thorough 
understanding of both quantum abilities and the needs of the 
dairy operations [16]. Real quantum advantage requires a 
problem selection and  stringent benchmarking guidelines, 
but there are no defined standards of quantum superiority in 
the agricultural setting [17], [19].  
B. Hardware and Technical Limitations  
Contemporary quantum computers experience severe 
qubit limitations, with available processors containing 
dozens to hundreds of qubits , while useful agricultural 
problems likely require thousands [19]. Khan et al. reported 
that quantum states are destroyed by circuit depth constraints 
due to decoherence before the completion of a computation 
[19]. The error rates of quantum are significantly  higher than 
the classical reliability rates, and decoherence corrupted state 
in microseconds [23]. The high reliability of the agricultural 
applications is needed because any miscalculation made in 
optimization has economic consequences , and the currently  
available quantum systems cannot fulfill this task [19]. 
Hardware is yet to be extremely easy with specialty 
cryogenic cooling and electromagnetic shielding 
requirements, and cloud facility services add network latency 
and frequent costs [25].  
C. Data Proces sing and Integration Issues  
The encoding of quantum data poses basic issues with the 
agricultural data [16]. The encoding of the classical data to a 
quantum state consists of encoding processes, which 
determine the efficiency of the algorithms, yet the enc oding 
process of high -dimensional agricultural data requires 
encoding processes that are difficult to perform in existing 
systems [18]. Maraveas et al. have found that encoding 
overhead can usually neutralize the theoretical advantages of 
speedup [16]. Acc ording to quantum measurements, to 
measure superposition states with probability , multiple 
executions are required to get the appropriate results [17]. 
The hybrid quantum -classical  integration introduces 
complexity to the architecture structure , and the wo rking of 
data transfer introduces latency to decrease real -time 
responsiveness [25].  
D. Implementation and Deployment Obstacles  
Quantum computing expertise requirements substantially 
exceed classical artificial intelligence needs, creating critical 
workforce shortages [19]. The development of quantum 
algorithms requires a high level of physics and mathematics, 
which is mostly beyond the reach of agricultural populations 
[18]. The financial aspect makes quantum -enhanced dairy 
systems economically impractical, a nd the cost of cloud 
services is much higher than the classical resources [20], 
[25]. Custom algorithm development involves talent that is 
special and needs premium compensation [19]. Agricultural 
organizations do not have internal quantum expertise to 
support the operations , and the third -party services are 
commercially unavailable [21], [25].  
E. Application -Specific Constraints  
There are special challenges to milk yield prediction with 
quantum neural networks because the training process needs 
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:37 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
large circuit executions, which are not efficiently available 
on current computers [10], [17]. Temporal dependency 
modeling requires quantum architectures that are highly 
restricted by hardware constraints [14]. Quantum 
approximate optimization algorithms to optimize fe eds are 
faced with problem -specific constraints, and nutritional 
formulations are clumsy to transfer to quantum objective 
functions [4], [5]. The multi -objective optimization with the 
consideration of economic, environmental, and welfare 
issues demands alg orithms that are more of a theory [5]. The 
speed of processing required to achieve real -time adjustment 
is not possible using the existing hardware [4].  
Figure 2 shows a dataset -based architecture to AI -based 
dairy decision support, which demonstrates the flow of 
historical data on dairy processing to milk yield prediction 
and management -based decision support.  
 
Fig. 2.   Architecture of a dataset -driven AI -based dairy decision support 
system.  
VI. COMPARATIVE ANALYSIS  
In this part, a well-organized comparison of classical AI 
and quantum -enhanced systems is made on the main 
performance dimensions, which are applicable to the dairy 
farm application. The comparison compares the different 
paradigms based on such criteria as prediction accu racy, 
computational scalability, optimization ability , and 
deployment feasibility.  
A. Performance and Capability Comparison  
Classical machine learning has proven to exhibit mature 
performance levels with established accuracy standards in 
agricultural applicat ions [9]. According to Zhang et al., 
neural network methods have always recorded an accuracy 
range of 80-85% in predicting milk yield in different dairy 
farming settings [9]. In the research of Liseune et al., 
correlation coefficients as high as 0.77 were reported in the 
prediction of lactation curves using deep learning in 
controlled experimental research, and they are mentioned 
here as the result of the previous research instead of the 
findings of the current research [10]. These levels of 
accuracy were d iscovered to be an improvement on 
traditional statistical methods , as well as having  limits of 
performance that cannot  be substantially exceeded by 
increasing the volumes of data and the complexity of the 
model  [13].  Figure 3 conceptually compares classica l AI and 
quantum -enhanced AI pipelines and shows that quantum -
enhanced pipelines differ in terms of computational 
processing stages , not empirical performance.  
 
Fig. 3.  Conceptual comparison between classical AI and quantum -
enhanced AI processing pipelines.

AI AND  QUANTUM -ENHANCED SYSTEMS

Systems  Quantum -Enhanced 
Systems  
Milk Yield Prediction  
Model Maturity  80–85% (field -
tested)  Up to 97% (benchmark 
studies) [18]  
Model Maturity  Commercially

level

Note: The values of performance presented in Table I are 
compiled from previously  published benchmark and 
experimental studies, and are presented solely for 
comparison. No empirical evaluation or experimental 
validation was performed as part of this work.  
The comparative performance analysis , as presented in 
Table I , is based on values reported in previous 
benchmarking and experime ntal studies. According to the 
existing literature, it is stated that quantum -enhanced models 
are reported to be more accurate in optimization than 
classical AI -based systems under controlled laboratory or 
simulated conditions [17],[18]. The reported benef its are 
largely achieved using clearly -defined benchmark problems 
and neuroinformatics datasets, and not using agricultural or 
dairy farming datasets, which limits their direct extrapolation 
to the dairy setting [23]. Hence, even though  the reported 
perfor mance capacity of quantum -enhanced models is not 
exploited to the fullest extent, the constraints on their 
application in dairy farming are the availability and cost of 
hardware.  
B. Implementation Feasibility Analysis  
The classical and quantum approaches vary in the basic 
requirements of computational resources [23]. Classical 
artificial intelligence requires large computing resources to 
train deep neural networks , but can be run effectively on 
commodity server resources during inference tasks [ 20]. 
Quantum computing needs dedicated cryogenics and 
electromagnetic isolation , which do not allow installation on 
a farm [16]. Quantum access in the cloud creates dependency 
in the operations on external services and network 
connectivity [25].  
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:37 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
TABLE II.  IMPLEMENTATION FEASIBILITY ASSESSMENT MATRIX  
Implementation Factor  Classical AI  Quantum -Enhanced  Deployment Timeline  
Hardware Requirements  Standard servers, GPUs  Cryogenic facilities, specialized 
quantum processors  Immediate vs. 5 -10 years  
Infrastructure Cost  Moderate ($10K -$100K)  Extremely high ($100M+ 
facility or cloud access)  Current vs. Future reduction expected  
Expertise Required  Data science, agricultural 
knowledge  Quantum physics, computer 
science, agriculture  Readily available vs. Severe shortage  
Software Maturity  Mature frameworks 
(TensorFlow, PyTorch)  Early -stage tools, limited 
documentation  Production -ready vs. Research prototype  
Integration Complexity  Moderate (existing farm 
systems)  High (qua ntum -classical hybrid 
architecture)  Months vs. Years  
Data Processing  Real-time sensor streams  Batch processing with encoding  
overhead  Operational vs. Experimental  
Scalability  Horizontal scaling with 
diminishing returns  Qubit -limited but theoretically 
exponential  Proven vs. Theoretical  
Maintenance Requirements  Routine IT support  Specialized quantum expertise  Manageable vs. Prohibitive  
Commercial Availability  Widely deployed  Research institutions only  Global vs. Limited geographic access  
 
The feasibility study is based on present technological 
preparedness and infrastructure needs and not quantified 
deployment results, thus emphasizing realistic limitations 
that relate to quantum -enhanced systems. Data handling 
capabilities present distinctly different challenge patterns [3]. 
Classical systems are useful in processing heterogeneous 
agricultural data streams using predefined preprocessing 
pipelines [29]. Quantum systems have a problem with the 
classi cal overhead of data encoding that often cancels out 
theoretical speedup benefits [16]. The data stream processing 
needs in real -time are incompatible with the batch -mode 
quantum circuit execution architectures [25].  
The complexity of implementation is sig nificantly 
different across paradigms [29]. Applications of classical AI 
use large software ecosystems such as TensorFlow and 
PyTorch that are well -documented  and have community 
support [20]. Quantum computing needs specific knowledge 
of quantum mechanics and circuit design that agricultural 
societies hardly have [19]. The software tools are still 
infantile and poorly documented , with a small user base [18].  
C. Scalability and Cost Considerations  
Scalability : The paradigms have fundamental 
differences in characteristics [24]. Classical methods utilize 
parallel computing infrastructure to achieve horizontal 
scalability , but with decreasing problem complexity [13]. 
Quantum computing provides conceptual exponential time 
speedup on particular pr oblem types but faces physical qubit 
bottlenecks limiting existing systems [19].  Economic 
analysis shows very sharp differences in the deployment 
costs [20]. Classical artificial intelligence systems have 
moderate start-up costs of between ten thousand and  one 
hundred thousand dollars and manageable  operation costs 
[22]. The cost of accessing quantum computing is quite high 
compared to classical resources , and the returns on 
investment are uncertain [25]. The quantum algorithm 
development requires very spec ialized skills that require 
high compensation [19].  
D. Technology Readiness Assessment  
Classical artificial intelligence technologies 
demonstrate high readiness levels with extensive operational 
deployment experience across dairy farms worldwide [21]. 
Baldin et al. reported industrial applications with 99.8% precision breeding program accuracy and cost savings with 
automated systems of a measurable amount [21]. The 
success of the implementation is due to the developed 
software frameworks, the network of commun ity support , 
and the best practices documented [20].  Quantum computing 
is still at first stages of development in terms of practical 
agricultural use  [25]. The hardware limitations , such as the 
low number of qubits, immaturity of the software 
ecosystem, an d integration issues , do not allow the 
operational deployment [16]. Khan et al. have found that the 
majority of quantum agriculture investigations involve 
theoretical but not real -life applications [19].  
VII. DISCUSSION  
This section is based on the comparative analysis and 
interprets the results within the context of the management of 
dairy farms and the adoption of technology. The discussion 
touches on the practical implications for stakeholders, trade -
offs between the t wo paradigms and a way forward to 
sustainable precision livestock management is discussed.  
A. Key Findings and Implications  
This comparative analysis reveals fundamentally distinct 
challenge profiles between classical artificial intelligence -
based and quantum -enhanced computational approaches for 
dairy farming applications [24]. Classical systems show 
operational maturity and have known levels of prediction 
accuracy of 80-85%  and have a long history of commercial 
deployment , but have computational scalability limits and 
accuracy improvement limits that cannot be significantly 
overcome by extra resources [9], [10]. Quantum systems 
have hypothetical benefits to particular classes of 
optimization problems but have significant limitations on 
hardware accessibility,  system integration, and deployment 
issues that do not permit real -world agricultural applications 
in the near future [19], [25].  Comparison of performance 
shows that quantum computing results are obtained  under 
well-managed experimental settings with buil t-in benchmark 
problems as opposed to real dairy farm operational settings 
[17]. Classical systems offer the accuracy level needed to 
support operational decision -making  in the present -day 
agricultural setting [9]. The reported fifty -fold speedup of 
quantu m algorithms is not practically relevant due to the 
extreme accessibility limitations of quantum hardware and 
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:37 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
implementation complexities of integration that cannot be 
deployed  [18], [23].  
B. Practical Recommendations  
The classical artificial intelligence sys tem adoption 
should be promoted as the relevant immediate strategy by 
dairy farm operators and the providers of agricultural 
technologies [21]. The neural networks, deep learning 
methods , and ensemble modelers provide a confident milk 
production prediction  and feed optimization feature that can 
be deployed with the current technology infrastructure [20]. 
Stoilova and Stoilov proved that strategic investments in data 
collection infrastructure, sensor networks, and computing 
resources that implement classical  artificial intelligence 
activities create direct operational returns [31].  
Developers of technologies and research institutes must 
seek to create hybrid computational structures that are ready 
to mature  to quantum technology but are still useful in 
practi cal terms by classical means [25]. Rexhepi et al. 
focused on the creation of quantum -ready data architecture 
patterns, the investigation of quantum algorithms to 
agricultural problems, and the development of 
interdisciplinary skills that will prepare organ izations to 
future quantum opportunities without reducing the present 
operational capacities [6].  The basic research should be 
directed towards this area of quantum agriculture application, 
but the researchers should not ignore long deployment 
timeframes t hat are not compatible with shorter agricultural 
planning periods [30]. Investments in research programs 
should focus on the development of basic knowledge, 
training of scientists who are interdisciplinary , and the 
establishment of validation frameworks in stead of the early 
operational system implementation [19].  
C. Future Research Directions  
Critical research gaps require attention for advancing 
both computational paradigms [11]. Classical systems 
require better high -dimensional data processing 
methodologies,  nonlinear biological relationship 
representation, and real -time multi -objective optimization 
features [14]. The creation of quantum computing requires 
the development of hardware, the creation of agriculture -
specific algorithms, and comprehensive validati on systems 
that prove the true benefits over classical methods [19].  
Hybrid quantum -classical architectures are emerging 
research areas that feature the advantages of quantum 
computing with the maturity of classical systems [25]. 
Tadeschi also promoted the  methodical exploration of the 
sub-problem peculiarities of dairy farming that could be 
improved with the help of quantum processing without 
abandoning classical elements in data processing and 
interpretation of the results [25].  
D. Study Limitations  
The stud y uses literature synthesis as the research 
methodology instead of empirical research, which limits the 
conclusions to the issues reported in the published academic 
literature [24]. The fast technological change in the field of 
artificial intelligence and quantum computing implies that 
the results are indicative of the current possibilities and not 
the future possible directions [18]. The fact that only limited 
direct comparative studies have been conducted between the 
classical and the quantum methods on t he same agricultural 
problem limits the precision of the performance assessment 
[23].  The literature in agricultural quantum computing is still limited, and the majority of studies have focused on 
theoretical possibilities of the algorithm instead of 
demon stration of practical implementation  
VIII. CONCLUSION  
This study conducted a systematic comparative analysis 
of classical AI and quantum -enhanced approaches for milk 
yield forecasting and feed optimization in dairy farming. A 
systematic literature review and multi -dimensional analysis 
have led to the construc tion of a systematic framework that 
can be used to evaluate the two paradigms in terms of 
computational efficiency, accuracy of predictions, 
scalability, optimization potential, and implementation. The 
analysis has revealed specific profiles of challenges:  classical 
machine learning systems can make operational predictions 
with accuracy up to 80 -85% and have deployment -ready  
scalability, but are limited to feed optimization strategies that 
remain static and multi -objective processing under real -time 
conditi ons. Theoretically better in combinatorial 
optimization, quantum -enhanced systems are limited to 
experimental applications because of the immaturity of 
hardware, qubit decoherence, and the cost of the 
infrastructure , which  is prohibitive, and have not been  
validated specifically for agriculture. The comparative 
analysis makes clear that classical AI is the practical short -
term answer to farm -level adoption , whereas quantum 
computing has a transformational long -term potential 
awaiting technological advanceme nt. The results are 
evidence -based practices that can guide stakeholders going 
through transitions in precision livestock management. The 
future studies need to take three directions. To begin with, 
the classical AI advancements must be aimed at the 
dynami cally multi -objective optimization and explainable AI 
systems to enhance the trust of the farmers. Second, 
agricultural quantum research must focus on qubit stability, 
the development of algorithms specific to dairy, and 
validation using actual farm data. Third, hybrid quantum -
classical architectures are an area over which systematic 
research is justified, using classical computing to make real -
time decisions and delegate certain optimization sub -
problems to quantum processors as hardware capabilities are 
improved. These hybrid systems have provided the best way 
forward to sustainable and next-generation  dairy production, 
which can sustain the future global food security demands.

ABSTRACT Accurate electricity demand forecasting is critical for efﬁcient energy management in regions
affected by rapid urbanization, grid instability, and load shedding. The traditional forecasting methods are
inadequate when it comes to the short-term variation due to weather conditions, socio-economic activities,
and unforeseen events. This paper introduces a deployable, statistically grounded electricity demand fore-
casting model that incorporates meteorological, calendar-based, and grid operational information, such as
temperature, humidity, rainfall, daylight hours, festive days, past consumption, and load shedding data. To
learn about the complex non-linear and temporal relationships in daily electricity demand, Random Forest
and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) frameworks are used and to facilitate the implementation of the
model in near real-time , the framework uses a FastAPI-based backend and a Flutter-based mobile app to
visualize the predictions. Resistance to sensor noise and non-observable types of events is improved by
integrating contextual features and outlier-aware data preprocessing. The experimental results on a dataset
with a time span of 7 years (2017–2024) indicate that the LSTM model is superior to the traditional models,
where the RMSE is reduced from 7.43 to 4.84 and the MAPE from 14.76% percent to 8.2%. The proposed
system provides a useful and scalable solution in the dynamic power grid environment for real-time electricity
demand forecasting.
INDEX TERMS Energy, urbanization, load-shedding, LSTM, mobile application.
I. INTRODUCTION
Load shedding affects the economic operations, which vary
in magnitude in different parts of the world. In India, the
fuel shortage resulted in up to 12-hour blackouts in the 2022
energy crisis [1],[2]. The power outages are 6 hours a day inSouth Africa due to a 14-year power crisis, whereas the power
outages in Pakistan are 12-15 hours in average, aggravated
by the reliance on imported fuel sources [3],[4]. There are
also long power outages in Sri Lanka and Lebanon caused
by the breakdown of the energy systems, and Bangladesh is

FERDAUS ET AL.: COMPREHENSIVE ELECTRICITY DEMAND FORECASTING WITH A CUSTOM MULTI-DIMENSIONAL DATASET
TABLE 1. Load Shedding Statistics in Different Countries [1],[3]
struggling with the power shortage of 1500 MW, which is a
heavy burden on the rural land and agricultural integration [1],
[5]. A summary of these challenges is presented in Table 1.
Effective energy management is essential for economic
development. With global power demand expected to grow
at 2.1% annually from 2025 to 2040, driven by urbaniza-
tion, this rise will inevitably lead to challenges such as grid
instability and economic losses due to wasted energy [6].
Conventional statistical models like ARIMA and linear re-
gression are often inadequate in capturing the nonlinear and
dynamic nature of electricity demand [2],[4],[7]. While
machine learning methods offer more ﬂexibility and preci-
sion [5],[8],[9], many existing models focus on long-term
predictions and fail to account for real-time ﬂuctuations in
demand, particularly in the context of load shedding [10].O n e
major challenge remains the integration of Demand Response
(DR) programs with real-time forecasting, as DR requires
accurate short-term predictions to adjust generation, storage,
and consumption patterns dynamically. However, traditional
models struggle to capture socio-economic factors, weather
variations, and unexpected grid events, which are essential
for effective DR execution [11]. To address this gap, we
propose a data-driven forecasting framework that supports
DR operations, enhances grid reliability, and minimizes the
risk of load-shedding. The recent research investigated fur-
ther deep learning models regarding electricity load prediction
such as attention-based and Transformer models capable of
sufﬁciently addressing long-range temporal correlations in
time-series data. Transformer-based forecasting models have
shown promising results in terms of performance because
they can process sequential data without using recurrent
structures. Moreover, hybrid methods that use traditional
machine learning models with deep neural networks have
become popular due to their capability to combine nonlin-
ear feature association with time learning mechanisms. These
advancements underline the need for smart forecasting mod-
els that can address the challenges of dynamic electricity
demand patterns in today’s electricity systems. Driven by
these developments, this paper explores machine learning as
well as deep learning methods of electricity demand predic-
tion on a multi-dimensional dataset.
This paper presents a data-driven framework for daily elec-
tricity demand prediction using Random Forest (RF) andLong Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks. Our approach
models intricate consumption behaviors and the key factors
inﬂuencing electricity demand prediction [12]. The system
also integrates a mobile application for real-time monitoring
and adaptive responses to demand ﬂuctuations [13].
The key objectives and ﬁndings of this study are as follows:rObjective 1: To develop an efﬁcient predictive model
that incorporates weather, socio-economic factors, and
historical consumption patterns.rObjective 2: To apply RF and LSTM models to capture
the complex, non-linear characteristics of electricity de-
mand.rObjective 3: To evaluate the model’s performance using
MAE, MAPE, RMSE, and R-squared, showing signiﬁ-
cant improvements over traditional methods.rObjective 4: To design a dynamic and ﬂexible frame-
work, with a mobile app to visualize predictions in
real-time.
This paper provides an efﬁcient and scalable solution for
managing daily electricity consumption in rapidly developing
regions, with a focus on improving real-time forecasting ca-
pabilities and supporting Demand Response programs.
The remaining part of the paper is provided as follows:
Section 2 is the backgroud and fundamentals; Section 3
is reviews and related works; Section 4 is describing the
methodology; Section 5 is discussing experimental results and
comparisons and Section 6 is conclusion in future direction.
II. LITERATURE REVIEW
The necessity of electricity demand forecasting is becoming
very critical because of urbanization and energy reliance and
proper forecasting will ensure stability in the grid and long
term energy management. As opposed to conventional statis-
tical models which have been in wide use, ML and DL are
more appropriate in modeling nonlinear and dynamic con-
sumption patterns. Previous research such as [2],[4],[5],
[14], primarily used statistical and end-use based methods
that do not necessarily appreciate short term ﬂuctuations
as a result of weather, socio-economic and load shedding.
Lee and Cho [15] compared conventional and ML-based
models, showing that LSTM outperforms traditional meth-
ods in peak load forecasting. Similarly, Velasquez et al. [16]
1298 VOLUME 7, 2026
FIGURE 1. Proposed methodology for the electricity demand prediction system. The diagram illustrates the overall workﬂow from data collection to
real-time prediction.
highlighted the effectiveness of hybrid time series mod-
els that account for seasonality. Tang et al. [8]integrated
ML and blockchain for predictive energy trading in micro-
grids, enabling real-time forecasting. Alquthami et al. [17]
found that decision tree models, particularly those with gra-
dient boosting, outperformed other classiﬁers in smart grid
forecasting.
In isolated power systems, Matrenin et al. [18] demon-
strated that ensemble models combining SVR and random
forests reduce overﬁtting and improve accuracy. LSTM net-
works, as used by Torres et al. [19], achieved under 1.5% error
in short-term forecasting with hyperparameter optimization.
Recent work explores CNNs for feature extraction:
Yazici et al. [20] showed that 1D CNNs can rival recurrent
models, while Khan et al. [21]proposed a CNN-LSTM frame-
work that outperformed baseline models. Comparative studies
by Bilgili and Pinar [22] conﬁrmed LSTM’s superiority over
SARIMA in Türkiye, whereas Nazir et al. [23]introduced the
Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT), which improved accu-
racy but required complex tuning.
Hybrid and advanced models also show promise. Abu-
mohsen et al. [24] found GRUs performed best among recur-
rent networks. Mounir et al. [25] used BI-LSTM with EMD
for high-accuracy forecasting, albeit with high computational
cost. Fan et al. [26] combined EWT, CNN, RNN/LSTM, and
BOA, and González Grandón et al. [27] integrated ARIMA
with LSTM and regression, both achieving high accuracy but
with increased model complexity. El-Azab et al. [28] createda seasonal forecasting system based on ANFIS, LSTM and
GRU and ANN that has been shown to be effective in inte-
grating the relationship between temperature and forecasting.
However, there are numerous studies that do not consider
such important factors as load shedding, grid reliability, and
socio-economic conditions limiting real-world scenarios. To
counter this, we suggest a hybrid RF-LSTM scheme to capture
non-linearities and temporal patterns with real-time grid and
load-shedding data, and the outcomes are visualised using a
mobile application to implement the proposed system in rapid
urbanizing areas.
III. PROPOSED SYSTEM MODEL AND METHODOLOGY
The proposed system uses a process ﬂow for electricity de-
mand prediction, as shown in Fig. 1. The process starts
with the collection of data from various sources, such as
historical electricity demand, weather factors (e.g., temper-
ature, humidity), and socio-economic factors (e.g., holiday
calendar, population density). The data is then preprocessed
and feature engineered by cleaning the data (e.g., removing
missing values), normalising the data, and selecting features.
The preprocessed data is then processed through machine
learning algorithms, namely, Random Forest (RF) and Long
Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks, which can capture
linear and non-linear patterns in electricity consumption. The
system is expected to adapt to different patterns by using these
models to forecast electricity consumption based on historical
data and other factors such as weather and socio-economic

TABLE 2. Overview of Experimental Dataset Attributes
events. Additionally, the system is equipped with a mobile
app interface for monitoring demand variations. This applica-
tion helps utilities to dynamically adjust to demand variations
through real-time predictions and alerts. The model is evalu-
ated using performance metrics: MAE (Mean Absolute Error),
RMSE (Root Mean Square Error), MAPE (Mean Absolute
Percentage Error) and R2. These criteria guarantee the de-
mand forecasting predictions are accurate. Fig. 1provides a
glimpse into the proposed approach, showcasing the imple-
mentation of forecasting and demand response using the data
in the proposed system. This depicts the entire workﬂow from
data collection to data processing and prediction and real-time
adaption of electricity demand.
A. DATASET DESCRIPTION
1) DATA SOURCE
This dataset includes the data on historical electricity demand,
weather, and contextual data of the three sources namely:rMeteorological Data: Collected from BMD portal [29].rBPDB Loads: Daily electricity demand, peak loads and
load-shedding records of Bangladesh Power Develop-
ment Board [30].rTime and Date Website: Calendar holidays and festival
data of Time and Date [31].
The total contribution of all the sources is given in Table- 2
illustrates the daily values of temperature, humidity, thun-
derstorms, daytime duration, rainfall, festive days, historical
electrical demand, and load shedding in the period between
January 1, 2017 and June 31, 2024. To ensure experimental
reproducibility, the assembled data combines publicly avail-
able data on BMD, BPDB, and TimeandDate portals. The
data range covers the interval between January 1, 2017 and
June 30, 2024, which has adequate seasonal variations to
train and test. To preserve the temporal integrity of fore-
casting, the dataset was divided chronologically into training
(2017–2021), validation (2022) and testing (2023–mid 2024)
subsets.The time-dependent splitting method aids in avoiding
data leakage and enables the models to generalize on seasonal
demand changes and possible concept drift due to shifting
consumption patterns over time.
2) DATA FEATURES
The following data set is used in the study to understand the
factors affecting daily electricity demand, both meteorological
and non-meteorological factors:rDate (DD-MM-YY): Shows the day of the month on
which the data was being collected for each year.rTemperature (◦C):Integral ﬁxed value relating to the
average temperature on the day analyzed, expressed in
Celsius degrees.rHumidity (%): Persistent numeric variable, which mea-
sures the air relative humidity in terms of the percent.rSunshine (Hours): Discrete variable, which measures the
quantity of Elementary sunlight, that the patient was
exposed to, in hours for the day.rNight-time (Hours): Discrete variable, or total amount of
time of darkness throughout the day.rRainfall (mm): Continuous variable, representing a total
amount of the precipitate of the day in millimeters.rHazardous Weather (1/0): Categorical variable, it mea-
sures whether a location experiences risky weather con-
ditions, including torrential rainfall or powerful wind, in
one day or not.rThunderstorm (1/0): Measuring the probability of thun-
derstorms, and since it is a categorical variable with
two categories – the presence of thunderstorms and no
thunderstorms – we assign it the value of 1 and 0, re-
spectively.rFestival Day (1/0): Categorical variable, representing
whether the day is a festival or not; where 1 signiﬁes
yes for festival and 0 for no.rDemand (MW): Steady variable referring to the overall
energy consumption for the certain day and described in
megawatts (MW).rLoad Shed (MW): Quantitative data with the number
of load shedding (unserved demand) for the day in
megawatts (MW).
Due to this diverse range of variables, it becomes possible
carrying out an evaluation on the determinants of electricity
demand inclusive of weather conditions, societal activities
and the performance of the grid. Categorical variables like,
Thunderstorm ,Hazardous Weather , and Festival Day were
encoded under binary encoding where 1 denoted the occur-
rence of an event and 0 denoted the non-occurrence of an
event. This encoding method enables the machine learning
models to effectively encode contextual variables like extreme
weather conditions and social activities that can greatly im-
pact the electricity demand patterns.
B. DATA PREPROCESSING
Constructive data preprocessing guarantees the reliability of
the models through the improvement of the quality of the data,
the processing of the inconsistencies, and the preparation of
the time series forecasting data.
1) HANDLING MISSING DATA
The missing values in the data set are due to the logging
error or the missing record of the sources such as BPDB and
meteorological agencies, being addressed by:
1300 VOLUME 7, 2026
TABLE 3. Comparison of the Proposed LSTM-Based System With Existing Forecasting Approaches
rForward Fill: For temporal variables like electricity de-
mand or temperature, assuming continuity in trends over
short intervals.rMean Imputation: Applied to non-temporal features to
preserve overall data distribution while minimizing in-
formation loss.
In addition to addressing missing value treatment, noisy
observations due to sensor anomalies or irregularities in re-
porting were corrected by simple smoothing and validation
checks. Temporal continuity of electricity demand data was
maintained by ﬁltering sudden spikes in the data due to
recording anomalies prior to model training. These prepro-
cessing steps enhance the reliability of the forecasting models
when working with real-world monitoring data.
2) OUTLIER TREATMENT
Outliers due to power surges, extreme weather, or entry errors
were detected using the Interquartile Range (IQR) method.
Values beyond Q1−1.5×IQR or Q3+1.5×IQR were
normalized to reduce their inﬂuence during model training,
ensuring robust learning without distortion from anomalies.
C. FEATURE SCALING (MIN-MAX NORMALIZATION)
Feature scaling is a critical preprocessing step in machine
learning to ensure that all input variables contribute equally
to the model’s learning process. In this study, continuous fea-
tures such as temperature, humidity, and electricity demand
are scaled using Min-Max normalization, which transforms
the features to a range of [0, 1]. The formula for this transfor-
mation is:
x/prime=x−xmin
xmax−xmin(1)
Where xis the original feature value, and xminandxmaxrepre-
sent the minimum and maximum values of the feature in the
dataset. Scaling ensures that variables with large numerical
ranges, such as electricity demand (in megawatts), do not
dominate smaller-range features like temperature (in degrees
Celsius). This equalizes the contribution of all features, en-
hancing model convergence during training. Feature scaling
is particularly beneﬁcial for models like Random Forest and
Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks, which are sen-
sitive to the scale of input features. By stabilizing training
FIGURE 2. Histogram of numerical features for electricity forecasting. This
image shows the distributions of the training features, including
temperature, humidity, rainfall and daily electricity demand, which are
important for the forecasting models. These features are essential to
explain electricity demand and its seasonal and weather variability.
and improving prediction accuracy, feature scaling becomes
an essential part of our preprocessing pipeline.
D. EXPLORATORY DATA ANALYSIS
In thsi section, the effect that the various features have on
electricity demand will be analyzed, which will assist in deter-
mining signiﬁcant predictors, and understanding of the model
will be enhanced to improve the accuracy of predictions.
1) FEATURE DISTRIBUTION ANALYSIS
The distribution of features plays a crucial role in the per-
formance of the models since the presence of patterns in the
variables determines how a model will be able to learn, its
accuracy to predict and how sensitive it is to outliers. The
Fig.2shows important numerical characteristics, such as tem-
perature, humidity, rainfall and daily electricity demand.
The temperature and humidity is also near normally dis-
tributed with seasonal extremes whereas rainfall is highly
skewed with frequent low values and occasional extreme
heavy precipitation which may lead to sudden demand peaks.
The seasonal trend of electricity demand is vivid with high

FIGURE 3. Correlation of features with electricity demand.
demands during the summer seasons because of cooling re-
quirements and low demands during winter seasons because
of weather and ﬂuctuations in seasons.
The perceived seasonal patterns of demand imply the sig-
niﬁcant role of capturing the time-dependencies in forecasting
models. The demand of electricity is higher in summer due
to the high cooling demand and lower in winter due to the
relatively low demand.The several years of historical data
incorporated in the models enable them to learn repetitive
seasonal trends and enhance their resilience to changes in
electricity consumption over time.
These distributions are important to analyze in order to
preprocess. The skewed variables are not allowed to affect
the models disproportionately through the use of appropriate
normalization, transformation, and outliers, which increase
the strength and accuracy of both Random Forest and LSTM.
2) CORRELATION ANALYSIS
Pearson correlation was used to determine the strength of re-
lations between features and their effect on demand. In Fig. 3,
the heatmap shows that the extent of correlation between fea-
tures and electricity demand.
Key ﬁndings include:rTemperature: Moderate positive relation ( r=0.50), cor-
responding to the use of cooling systems more often on
hot weather.rHumidity: Weak positive correlation ( r=0.03), indicat-
ing not having any signiﬁcant impact.rRainfall: Non-signiﬁcant correlation( r=0.01), indicat-
ing weak effect.rFestive Days: Weak negative correlation ( r=−0.11),
because of less industrial and commercial activity.
These correlations guide the choice of features as well as
improve the performance of the forecast model.
3 ) F E A T U R EI M P O R T A N C EA N A L Y S I S
In order to gain more insight into the role played by each
feature in the prediction, the feature importance analysis was
conducted using the Random Forest model. Random Forestestimates have an important property in which each feature
is evaluated by the amount of impurity reduction achieved
by multiple decision trees. This analysis aids in determining
the most critical predictors that inﬂuence electricity demand.
These ﬁndings demonstrate that meteorological variables,
including temperature and seasonal indicators are predomi-
nant in demand forecasts, followed by contextual variables,
including festival days and grid-related variables. The contri-
butions of these features can also be used to enhance model
interpretability and assist in decision-making by energy man-
agement systems.
E. MACHINE LEARNING ALGORITHMS
Electricity demand forecasting plays a vital role in the
efﬁcient operation of power systems, particularly in plan-
ning generation, reducing costs and enhancing energy se-
curity [32]. Machine learning approaches have shown their
effectiveness in capturing the underlying nonlinear relation-
ships in electricity demand data. Different methods have been
used to predict electricity demand, each with its own merits
depending on the dataset and temporal features of the elec-
tricity demand.
These models include traditional statistical and machine
learning models such as Linear Regression and Polynomial
Regression, to more sophisticated models such as Random
Forest, Gradient-Boosting, Auto Regressive Integrated Mov-
ing Average (ARIMA) and deep learning models including
Long short-term memory (LSTM) networks. The choice of
model depends on the interpretability, accuracy and abil-
ity to capture time-series data characteristics of the model
[33].
Random Forest and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM)
models are applied separately, not as a hybrid or ensemble
model in this study. The objective is to evaluate their rel-
ative performance in terms of capturing various features of
electricity demand data. Random Forest speciﬁcally works
well with nonlinear relationships between multiple variables
used as inputs, whereas LSTM networks are better suited
to learning time-dependent and sequential relationships that
exist in time-series data. Through the comparative analysis of
these models, this research will offer a better insight into the
relative advantages of the classical machine learning and deep
learning methods to a multi-dimensional electricity demand
forecasting dataset.
F. PROPOSED MODEL FRAMEWORK
This algorithm is most effective in time series forecasting
scenarios and is highly encouraged in these cases. It is well
capable of capturing the dynamic nature and the conse-
quent relationships that exist between past trends and future
trends. In this research, we adopt the Long Short-Term Mem-
ory (LSTM)-based method to forecast electricity demand, as
shown in the Fig. 4.
1302 VOLUME 7, 2026
FIGURE 4. Proposed electricity demand forecasting system architecture. The framework combines various input characteristics, such as historical
demand, weather conditions (temperature, humidity, rainfall), and calendar data, and then preprocesses it with data cleaning, Min–Max normalizat ion
and sequence generation. The processed data are subsequently inputted into a deep learning model of a two-layer LSTM network and a machine learning
model of a Random Forest regressor. Lastly, the system measures performance of forecasting through MAE, MAPE, RMSE and R2to produce precise
predictions of daily electricity demand.
FIGURE 5. Forecast demand with ARIMA.
1) INCORPORATION OF TIME DEPENDENCIES AND
EXTERNAL VARIABLES
The LSTM model is trained using past electricity demand
data along with meteorological and socio-economic factors
such as temperature, humidity, rainfall, festive days, and load
shedding records. These external characteristics are added to
values of historical demand at each time step in order to
give more contextual information and enhance the forecasting
accuracy.
FIGURE 6. Forecast demand with Prophet.
G. HYPERPARAMETER OPTIMIZATION
To achieve the best performance of the models, hyperpa-
rameter optimization was performed through a grid search
approach with validation evaluation. In the case of the
Random Forest model, the number of trees, the maximum
tree depth and the minimum number of samples needed to
split a node were systematically tested to identify the best
conﬁguration. In the same way, various hyperparameters of
the LSTM model were experimentally optimized to enhance
VOLUME 7, 2026 1303
FERDAUS ET AL.: COMPREHENSIVE ELECTRICITY DEMAND FORECASTING WITH A CUSTOM MULTI-DIMENSIONAL DATASET
FIGURE 7. Forecast demand with LSTM.
forecasting. The tuning was done by experimenting with var-
ious values of learning rate, number of stacked LSTM layers,
batch size and dropout rate. In particular, the learning rate was
adjusted between 0.001 and 0.01, and the LSTM layers were
also adjusted from one to three layers. The batch size of 16,
32 and 64 was tested to trade-off between training efﬁciency
and model convergence. Moreover, dropout regularization
was used between 0.1 and 0.5 to reduce overﬁtting in the
training process. Adam optimizer was chosen because it has
an adaptive learning rate and converges steadily. The ﬁnal
hyperparameter conﬁguration of the two models were chosen
according to the least validation error of the training process.

1) STATISTICAL SIGNIFICANCE TESTING
A statistical signiﬁcance test was performed between the pre-
dicted output of the models to ensure that the performance
gains obtained by the proposed LSTM-based model are sta-
tistically meaningful. A paired t-test was conducted in this
study to compare the prediction errors of LSTM with the base-
line models, including the Random Forest and ARIMA. The
null hypothesis states that there are no signiﬁcant differences
between forecasting performances of the compared models.
The results of the statistical tests indicated that the decrease
in RMSE and MAPE by the LSTM model is statistically sig-
niﬁcant at the 95% conﬁdence level ( p<0.05). This conﬁrms
that the observed rise in forecasting accuracy is not by chance
but is due to the fact that LSTM is more effective in capturing
time-sensitive information in electricity demand data.
B. COMPARISON WITH EXISTING FORECASTING MODELS
As shown in Table 4the proposed LSTM-based forecasting
framework consistently achieves better prediction accuracy
compared with these baseline models. This demonstrates the
ability of LSTM to effectively capture both temporal dynam-
ics and nonlinear interactions among multiple environmental
and contextual variables. The experimental ﬁndings prove
that the LSTM model was better than all the tested baseline
models. In particular, LSTM had the lowest MAPE of 8.20%,
RMSE of 4.84 and MAE of 3.05 with a R-Squared value
of 39.46%. Comparatively, the Random Forest model had a
greater MAPE of 14.76% and RMSE of 7.43, representing
poorer forecasting accuracy. Gradient Boosting recorded the
same result with a MAPE of 14.83% but it was still worse
than LSTM. The classical regression models, including Poly-
nomial and Linear Regression, were much less effective, with
MAPE values of 15.18% and 23.04%, respectively indicat-
ing that they do not include the nonlinear demand dynamics.
ARIMA model performed the worst with the largest MAPE
value of 30.37% and lowest R-Squared value of 16.27% due to
