the fact that it is based on linear assumptions and does not ade-
quately account to nonlinear trends in electricity demand data.
Comprehensively, he LSTM model minimized the RMSE
from 7.43 (Random Forest baseline) to 4.84 and enhanced the
MAPE by 14.76% to 8.20%. These gains were also veriﬁed
through statistical signiﬁcance tests, which conﬁrmed that the
performance improvement of the LSTM model is statistically
signiﬁcant. This ﬁnding indicates that deep learning models
are useful in the modeling of both nonlinear relationships and
time-dependent relationships in electricity demand prediction.
C. VALIDATION OF CHOOSING LSTM
The rationale for using the LSTM model to predict the daily
electricity demand includes the following aspects mainly;
First, LSTM is speciﬁcally designed for sequence data, and
second, it takes into account the temporal dependence that is
inherently associated with time series forecasting tasks. Un-
like conventional machine learning algorithms which might
not capture time-varying dynamics appropriately, LSTM is
a kind of RNN that aims to overcome some of these earlier
models’ ﬂaws, including gradient vanishing. This capability is
especially important for predicting the electric load since elec-
tricity consumption to a great extent has a cyclic, periodic, as
well as rhythmic nature that must be taken into consideration
for short- and long-term periods [37].
For this reason, in the given study, the LSTM model was
chosen as it tends to deﬁne such temporal dependencies in
electricity demand. In order to prove that we indeed chose
LSTM the right way, we compared the LSTM performances
with other models like Linear regression, Polynomial re-
gression, Random forest, Gradient Boosting, and ARIMA.
LSTM performed better than all other mentioned models in
terms of MAPE, RMSE, MAE, and R-squared [38]. Addition-
ally, the empirical evaluation with classical statistical models
(ARIMA and Prophet) and machine learning algorithms (Ran-
dom Forest and Gradient Boosting) veriﬁes that LSTM-based
forecasting has lower errors. This is also conﬁrmed by sta-
tistical signiﬁcance tests, and supports the validity of the
presented approach.
D. GRAPHICAL COMPARISON OF PREDICTIONS
This section compares the performance of the LSTM model
with traditional time series models like ARIMA and Prophet
for daily electricity demand forecasting. The comparison
highlights the advantages of LSTM, particularly in capturing
long-range temporal dependencies and non-linear relation-
ships, which traditional models struggle with.
The statistical ARIMA model is suitable for linear and
stationary data but not for non-stationary variables and sea-
sonality in electricity demand. It has difﬁculty capturing
variability and dynamic change, as depicted in Fig. 5and
high RMSE and MAPE, making it less suitable for predicting
electricity demand. Prophet performs better with time series
that have seasonality and trends. It is more effective in deal-
ing with seasonality than ARIMA, but performs moderately
as shown in Fig. 6but cannot capture complex interactionswith other variables such as temperature and humidity, lim-
iting its predictive power compared to other complex ML
models. LSTM effectively models temporal dependencies and
non-linear trends, capturing long-range effects of temperature,
holidays, and dynamic factors. Its gating mechanisms retain
relevant past information, providing high accuracy and adapt-
ability in electricity demand forecasting, as shown in Fig. 7.
E. MOBILE APPLICATION
A Flutter mobile app was developed to visualize electric-
ity demand forecasts.It provides a user-friendly interface for
visualizing current trends and forcasts. Users input param-
eters, which the FastAPI backend processes using a trained
model, storing predictions in a SQL database for real-time ac-
cess [46]. The mobile visualization frontend allows real-time
monitoring of electricity demand forecasts via a user-friendly
interface. The electricity forecasting models are hosted on a
cloud server where predictions are made. The forecasts are
then passed to the mobile app via a lightweight RESTful API.
To achieve optimal system performance, the inference time of
the deployed LSTM model was measured to be in the range of
a few hundred milliseconds per forecast, which is adequate for
near real-time electricity demand monitoring. Furthermore, a
data synchronization process is established between the cloud
server and mobile edge device to reduce communication costs
while keeping forecasting results up-to-date. This approach
allows users to obtain predictions in a timely manner without
high computational cost on the mobile device. Fig. 8repre-
sents the app’s intuitive interface, displaying forecasts in a
clear format so that users can easily interpret the forecasted
information based on real-time updates. Integration of Flut-
ter, FastAPI, and SQL ensures smooth data ﬂow, real-time
updates, and a responsive user experience, enabling efﬁcient
monitoring of energy consumption.
F. DISCUSSION
In this paper, the study suggests a data-driven electricity
demand forecast model based on LSTM networks, with
consideration of the historical consumption, meteorological
variables and situational factors such as calendar events to
achieve non-linear and temporal correlations. The LSTM per-
formed better than the classical approaches with MAPE of
8.21% and RMSE of 4.84%, whereas Random Forest and
Gradient Boosting were capable of capturing non-linear rela-
tionships but failed to capture temporal patterns and ARIMA
and Prophet were limited by non-stationarity trends.The use
of temperature, humidity, and the information about festive
days was useful in enhancing accuracy and showed how
vital feature selection and preprocessing can be. The main
strengths are LSTM capability to capture time-dependency,
frequency of incorporating contextual and weather-related
features and real-time observation and robust preprocessing
to overcome outliers and skewed distributions. On the other
hand, limitations include dependence on a daily aggregation
of data, overﬁtting, as well as dependence on external data.
Further research will include the study of faster frequency
1306 VOLUME 7, 2026
data of the IoT, hybrid architectures, scalable training, multi-
modality features, and more sophisticated regularization to
enhance robustness. Future research will explore the use of
high-frequency data, hybrid models (LSTM with Random
Forest/CNN), incorporation of real-time data from IoT, and
enhancing computational efﬁciency for real-time application.
Further, more sophisticated models like GRU and Trans-
former will be investigated for better forecasting capability,
scalability and ﬂexibility in smart grid.
V. CONCLUSION
Here, we have shown the usefulness of a data-driven approach
to daily electricity demand forecasting with Long Short-Term
Memory (LSTM) networks. The model, which effectively
captures the non-linearity and periodicity of electricity de-
mand, shows superior performance compared to conventional
methods like ARIMA, Random Forest and Prophet. We have
improved the forecast accuracy by considering both weather,
calendar, and grid-related information including tempera-
ture, humidity, rainfall, daylight hours and load shedding.
The LSTM model, combined with a FastAPI server and
Flutter-based mobile app, enables near real-time forecasting,
providing a viable approach to smart grids and dynamic en-
ergy systems. The use of outlier-resistant data preprocessing
and contextual information enhances the model’s resilience
to sensor errors and unobservable events, making it more
reliable and effective in practice. Our experiments on a 7-year
(2017-2024) dataset reveal signiﬁcant model performance en-
hancements, with RMSE reducing from 7.43 to 4.84 and
MAPE from 14.76% to 8.2%, highlighting the practical bene-
ﬁts of this approach. While the existing model has limitations
in terms of data resolution, overﬁtting, and scalability, it of-
fers a robust and adaptable model for predicting electricity
demand. Future research will aim to improve the model’s
applicability through the use of high-frequency data, hybrid
models and Internet of Things (IoT) for better management of
dynamic energy systems.

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INTELLIGENT TRANSPORTATION SYSTEMS, VOL. 27, NO. 3, MARCH 2026 3443
Crowdsourced Delivery Problem With Couriers’
Preferences: A Data-Driven Method With
Logic-Based Benders Decomposition
Xuting Sun
 , Yue Hu, and Jiage Huo
Abstract —With the rapid development of food delivery plat-
forms, crowdsourced delivery mode has become popular due to its
cost-eectiveness and ﬂexibility. The crowdsourced couriers can
choose to accept or reject orders dispatched from the platform.
Such behavior not only aects the income of the couriers and the
customer service quality, but also brings about negative impacts
on the platform’s operational eciency and its reputation. It
is essential to analyze the preferences of the couriers so that
the platform can develop a “courier-friendly” and high ecient
delivery dispatching strategy. Therefore, our study ﬁrst uses
a real-world food delivery dataset to identify the factors that
inﬂuence crowdsourced couriers’ willingness to accept orders. In
particular, we apply an extreme gradient boosting tree (XGBoost)
to forecast the likelihood of the crowdsourced couriers accepting
the delivery tasks. We further integrate the likelihood of order
acceptance into a mixed-integer programming model to optimize
the dispatching decisions for the platform. A multi-criteria
objective function is proposed aimed at making a trade-o
between the labor cost, service quality as well as couriers’ welfare.
To solve the problem, a logic-based Benders decomposition
algorithm is proposed, and an ecient analytical cut is developed
to accelerate the algorithm. The numerical results verify that
the proposed solution approach has signiﬁcant advantages in
terms of computational eciency and solution quality compared
to the o-the-shelf solver. Moreover, it is identiﬁed that, for
on-demand delivery platforms, integrating courier preference
information into the courier dispatch system is a vital strategy
for achieving more ecient and cost-eective operations, as well
as an important pathway to achieve social sustainable operations.
Index Terms—On-demand food delivery, crowdsourcing, will-
ingness to accept orders, machine learning, logic-based Benders
decomposition.
I. INTRODUCTION
WITH the rapid development of e-commerce and local
life services, on-demand food delivery, which is a
typical online-to-oine (O2O) service [1], has become a
critical component of urban logistics. With the O2O service,
customers can choose products and place orders online, and
receive the ordered products oine [2]. The O2O platforms
Received 24 January 2025; revised 30 June 2025 and 4 October 2025;
accepted 28 November 2025. Date of publication 15 December 2025; date
of current version 10 March 2026. This work was supported in part by the
National Natural Science Foundation of China under Grant 72471132 and
Grant 72001130. The Associate Editor for this article was B. Du. (Corre-
sponding author: Jiage Huo.)
The authors are with the SILC Business School, Shanghai University,
Shanghai 201899, China (e-mail: sunxuting@shu.edu.cn; jchy2021@163.com;
huojiage@163.com).
This article has supplementary downloadable material available at
https://doi.org/10.1109/TITS.2025.3640137, provided by the authors.
Digital Object Identiﬁer 10.1109/TITS.2025.3640137serve as the foundation for on-demand food delivery, oering
fast point-to-point delivery services from stores to customers to
satisfy the demands of online consumers [3]. By June 2024,
the global user base of food delivery services has exceeded
3 billion,1accounting for approximately 60% of all netizens
worldwide. In the international market, multiple food delivery
platforms have rapidly expanded. For example, Uber Eats, one
of the most widely used food delivery platforms, generated
almost $12.2 billion in revenue in 2023.2DoorDash, the
market leader in the US, has achieved 60% of the US food
delivery market share by 2023.3In China, Meituan and Ele.me
hold more than 90% of the domestic food delivery market.4
With their large user base and ecient distribution network,
these food delivery platforms are pushing the industry forward.
Major food delivery platforms are continuously enhancing
their delivery time guarantees, with standard service windows
typically ranging from 30 minutes to an hour. Some platforms
have also introduced “Flash Delivery” services. In addition,
there are also compensation plans for delivery delays on
various platforms to guarantee the customer experience. The
compensation amount is decided by the order value and delay
duration, typically ranging from a few to several dozen yuan.
Consequently, optimizing operational costs while maintaining
service quality remains a critical challenge.
On-demand delivery becomes more and more popular due to
its convenience and consumers’ needs [4]. For the food deliv-
ery platform, the couriers can be mainly classiﬁed as dedicated
couriers and crowdsourced couriers [5], and crowdsourced
couriers are utilized to reduce the wait time of customers [6].
However, the voluntary order acceptance mechanism of crowd-
sourced couriers introduces signiﬁcant variability in order
fulﬁllment rates due to their order preferences [7]. Within
algorithm-driven food delivery systems, couriers frequently
suer order assignment uncertainty as well as workload pres-
sure [2]. This will further increase the uncertainty when
accepting orders. Our ﬁeld study of 51 couriers (information
of this survey can be found in Appendix IV of Supplementary
Material) reveals that nearly all crowdsourced couriers selec-
tively reject unsuitable orders. This ﬁnding is corroborated

3444 IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INTELLIGENT TRANSPORTATION SYSTEMS, VOL. 27, NO. 3, MARCH 2026
by our analysis of an instant delivery dataset,5which shows
that approximately 70% of couriers have rejected orders at
least once. Notably, rejected orders may be reassigned up to
17 times, with an average reassignment duration of 118.85
seconds before ﬁnal acceptance. From a sustainable operations
perspective, incorporating courier preferences into dispatching
decisions presents a dual beneﬁt: it enhances system eciency
while aligning with platforms’ broader social responsibility
objectives.
Therefore, in this study, we focus on crowdsourced deliv-
ery problem in the context of on-demand food delivery,
and incorporate the uncertainty of crowdsourced couriers’
order preferences (i.e., acceptance probability) into the static
scheduling model to optimize order assignment and rout-
ing decisions in a dispatch cycle. The speciﬁc workﬂow
is as follows: The platform ﬁrst collects orders and then
assigns them to dedicated couriers and crowdsourced couriers.
Crowdsourced couriers can choose to accept or reject orders,
while dedicated couriers must accept assigned orders. We ﬁrst
predict the order acceptance probability of each crowdsourced
courier and input the predicted values as a key parameter into
the optimization model, thereby improving overall delivery
system’s eciency. The main contributions are as follows:
(1) We identify eight features that inﬂuence crowd-
sourced courier order choices from a real delivery order
dataset. We classify couriers using K-Means++ and build
predictive models for each courier type to obtain accep-
tance probabilities. This guarantees that when dispatching
orders, the platform oers couriers a more customized work
experience.
(2) We develop an order assignment and routing model
considering courier preferences to optimize order assignment
strategies. To solve the model, we propose a logic-based
Benders decomposition (LBBD) algorithm, and solves the
original problem by decomposing it into order assignment and
order sequencing. Also, we design an eective analytical cut
to improve the algorithm’s performance.
(3) Our model and algorithm can signiﬁcantly reduce rejec-
tion rates and improve computational eciency. In addition,
sensitivity analysis reveals that the platform is in a dilemma
in terms of operational costs and service quality. That is,
when the platform is unwilling to re-dispatch orders frequently,
i.e., increase the rejection coecient, the proportion of orders
assigned to dedicated couriers increases. However, this will
lead to a surge in orders for dedicated couriers, which will
negatively aect the delivery eciency. Therefore, the plat-
form has to assign orders to crowdsourced couriers, potentially
increasing the dispatch times.
The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Sec-
tion IIreviews the relevant literature. Section IIIintroduces
the problem description and the mathematical formulation.
Section IVprovides the predictive model and the logic-
based Benders decomposition algorithm. Section Vshows
the numerical results. Finally, Section VIconcludes this
paper.
5Online available at https://connect.informs.org/tsl/tslresources/
datachallenge (accessed on Jan2025).II. L ITERATURE REVIEW
In this section, we ﬁrst conduct a comprehensive review
of crowdsourced delivery problem. We particularly focus on
the ﬁndings in areas such as compensation mechanisms and
routing optimization. Then, we review the research works that
are closely related to our work, namely the willingness to
accept orders in the context of crowdsourced delivery.
A. Crowdsourcing Delivery
Crowdsourcing has attracted widespread attention due to its
ﬂexibility and cost-eectiveness. The Challenges and oppor-
tunities of crowdsourced delivery planning have be reviewed
by Savelsbergh and Ulmer [8], [9]. Regarding compensa-
tion for crowdsourcing, Dahle et al. [10] introduced three
compensation schemes and discussed their impact. In the
ﬁrst compensation scheme, the company provides a ﬁxed
and equal compensation for each service request, the second
compensation is proportional to the travel cost from the pickup
location to the delivery location, and in the third scheme, the
compensation is proportional to the additional cost incurred
by the crowdsourcing driver’s detour. The results show that
increasing the complexity of the compensation scheme will
produce greater savings. Alnaggar et al. [11] provided a
dynamic matching policy for the compensation problem of
crowdsourcing drivers, which enabled crowdsourcing drivers
to obtain a speciﬁc level of compensation while maintaining
ﬂexibility in their working hours. They proposed a Markov
Decision Process (MDP) model to capture the dynamic and
stochastic characteristics, and then designed a value function
approximation algorithm to eciently solve the MDP models.
Extensive research has been conducted on the crowdsourced
delivery problem. With the emergence of crowdsourced logis-
tics, considering the challenges due to increased uncertainty
and dynamism, researchers have proposed dynamic routing
models and stochastic routing models that incorporate stochas-
tic demand or service time. Reyes et al. [12] introduced
the dynamic meal delivery routing problem and developed
a rolling-horizon repeated-matching algorithm to solve this
problem in real time. Arslan et al. [13] innovatively inves-
tigated the routing problem involving both ad hoc drivers and
dedicated vehicles. To address the matching between crowd-
sourcing and real-time tasks, they proposed a rolling horizon
framework and developed an exact solution approach based on
a matching formulation. Tao et al. [14] modeled the problem
as a multi-warehouse pickup problem with dynamic drivers.
They proposed an event-based rolling horizon approach, which
updates the delivery route plan by iteratively calling a re-
optimization process at each decision epoch. In the study
by Silva et al. [15], not only the availability of crowd-
sourced drivers is uncertain, but also for the arrivals of orders.
Therefore, they proposed a novel and exact solution method
to compute the optimal prior solution before uncertainty is
revealed. They then imposed probabilistic constraints to limit
infeasible routes caused by capacity exceedance. Torres et al.
[16] addressed the randomness of crowd drivers’ availability
by constructing a two-stage stochastic model and introduced
a branch-and-price algorithm to solve routes. For large-scale
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:42 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
SUN et al.: CROWDSOURCED DELIVERY PROBLEM WITH COURIERS’ PREFERENCES 3445
problems, a column generation heuristic algorithm was also
proposed. C ˆot´e et al. [17] proposed a two-stage formula-
tion combining with a sample average approximation (SAA)
method to address uncertainties in customer demand, location,
and service time. In the ﬁrst stage, delivery area time windows
are allocated, while the second stage employs an adaptive large
neighborhood search algorithm to optimize delivery routes.
B. Willingness to Accept Orders
There are a few studies in the literature that mention prob-
ability inference related to the willingness of crowdsourced
drivers to participate in last mile delivery, as well as the poten-
tial impact of such participation on delivery costs. The existing
literature mainly focuses on the factors that aect crowdsourc-
ing couriers’ acceptance behavior, while some studies focus
on path planning. Hou [18] found that the age, income, and
compensation per order are the most inﬂuential factors in the
decisions of crowdsourced couriers to accept or reject delivery
tasks through a questionnaire survey. Tao et al. [19] studied
three settings for driver preferences: the minimum daily order
quantity, the maximum order quantity allowed per trip, and the
proportion of detours. The ﬁndings indicate that among all the
design parameters of the O2O on-demand delivery system, the
maximum number of orders allowed per trip and the detour
ratio are crucial. Boysen et al. [20] investigated the impact
of driver capacity, maximum acceptable detour, and minimum
revenue per unit time on optimization models. They proposed
an eective exact algorithm based on Benders decomposition.
Archetti et al. [21] explored the vehicle routing problem
with occasional drivers, and the willingness of an occasional
driver to deliver goods depends on whether the delivery
location is too far from the driver’s destination. Gdowska
et al. [22] extended the research of Archetti et al. [21] by
suggesting that drivers do not always accept tasks assigned to
them. Therefore, they proposed a bi-level stochastic model.
They introduced probability to represent the delivery will-
ingness of each driver and solved the deterministic Vehicle
Routing Problem after the tasks were accepted. Barbosa et
al.[7]employed logistic regression to model the willing-
ness of crowdsourced couriers to undertake deliveries. This
model was utilized to establish a new compensation scheme
based on the current routing plans of dedicated ﬂeets and
the probability of crowdsourcing acceptance. A direct search
algorithm aimed at minimizing expected costs was adopted
to determine the reward values for crowdsourcing. Ausseil
et al. [23] proposed an uncertain dynamic matching problem
regarding the acceptance or rejection of bids in crowdsourcing.
They transformed the responses of crowdsourced drivers into
probabilities of accepting future bids and utilized this infor-
mation to optimize subsequent bidding decisions. The results
indicate that online approximation and the incorporation of
individual crowdsourced driver’s acceptance estimates into
order scheduling leads to a more ecient allocation. C ¸ ınar et
al.[6]introduced the crowdshipping assignment problem with
compensation-dependent acceptance probabilities. A mixed-
integer nonlinear model for generic acceptance probability
functions and an approximate linearization scheme was pro-
posed. Additionally, an exact two-stage solution algorithm wasdeveloped to separately handle compensation and assignment
decisions. Some studies have employed machine learning to
predict drivers’ order acceptance behaviors. Wang et al. [1]
used the extreme gradient boosting tree (XGBoost) model
to predict crowdsourcing preferences in food delivery and
designed an eective allocation sorting heuristic to generate
order allocation schemes. They found that pickup distance,
delivery cost, and remaining time are the main factors that
couriers consider when selecting orders from the recommen-
dation list, while delivery distance is not important. Tao
et al. [2]introduced two machine learning-based models to
predict the delivery speed and capacity of each crowdsourced
driver in real-time, enabling better order allocation and rout-
ing decisions to minimize total costs. Similar research has
emerged in the ridesharing domain. Horner et al. [24] devel-
oped a personalized menu to provide rideshare crowdsourced
drivers with autonomy in their choices, gaining insights into
drivers’ request preferences through their expressed willing-
ness to participate. The problem was addressed using the SAA
method, and the results showed that the proposed stochas-
tic optimization method outperformed current methods and
deterministic optimization methods. Timilsina & Silvestri [25]
investigated rideshare drivers’ preferences for various tasks
through spatial crowdsourcing, reinforcement learning and
reverse auction theory. Based on the preferences, personalized
lists are recommended to each driver. Then, drivers bid for
their favorite tasks on their list through a reverse auction
to achieve the optimal allocation of tasks. Yang et al. [26]
conducted a probabilistic modeling of order acceptance and
rejection behaviors among crowdsourced drivers to provide
personalized delivery suggestion menus. They introduced two
assortment optimization problems. For the disjoint problem,
a standard greedy algorithm was proposed, while greedy and
local search algorithms were employed for the joint assortment
problem.
Although there are some studies that analyze the willingness
of crowdsourced couriers to accept additional orders, the
literature focusing on the on-demand food delivery process
is scarce. Our research ﬁlls the gap regarding the modeling
of delivery orders acceptance behavior of the crowdsourced
couriers in the context of on-demand food delivery, which
has not yet been systematically explored. Although machine
learning has been widely applied to model and predict driver
behavior, this has primarily been within ridesharing. However,
in the context of on-demand food delivery, it lacks a deep
exploration of the couriers’ order acceptance behavior and its
impact on the crowdsourcing delivery system. Thus, our study
focuses on couriers’ order acceptance behavior by analyzing
various factors that aect their willingness to accept orders. In
addition, we construct a novel order assignment and routing
model to make a trade-o among operational cost, delivery
eciency, and couriers’ welfare.
III. P ROBLEM DESCRIPTION AND FORMULATION
A. Problem Description and Notations
In this section, an order assignment and routing problem in
the context of instant food delivery is formulated, in which
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:42 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
3446 IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INTELLIGENT TRANSPORTATION SYSTEMS, VOL. 27, NO. 3, MARCH 2026
both dedicated and crowdsourced couriers are considered.
According to the real business operations [27], dedicated
couriers are full-time employees of the delivery service plat-
form, while crowdsourced couriers are part-time employees.
Dedicated couriers cannot reject the orders dispatched by the
dispatch system, whereas crowdsourced couriers can reject
orders based on their preferences [6].
Speciﬁcally, whether a crowdsourced courier accepts the
dispatched orders is uncertain and multiple factors aect the
order acceptance behavior. According to Archetti et al. [21],
the willingness of a crowdsourced courier to accept an addi-
tional order depends on the information of the current orders
assigned to him/her. Besides, the information of the new order
(e.g. meal preparation time, distance between the restaurant
and customer) also aects the willingness of a crowdsourced
courier to accept the order [1]. Due to dierent preferences
of orders, dierent crowdsourced couriers will accept orders
based on their own preference, and will show dierent order
acceptance behaviors. The two kinds of historical information
aforementioned are utilized to predict the order acceptance
probability, which is incorporated into the crowdsourced deliv-
ery problem to consider the order acceptance behavior of each
crowdsourced courier.
For the platform under consideration, the operating cycle
has a length of T. Within the operating cycle [0;T], orders
are assigned to couriers with their routes in a time slot 
(e.g., 1 minute). Assuming Tis a multiple of , the set of
time period indices within the operating cycle is denoted by

. In this study, we focus on the static order

At each dispatch cycle t2 , there are two kinds of decisions to

replanning for each courier with his/her currently dispatched
orders and the unﬁnished orders in the delivery process.
The instant delivery network can be represented by a
network graph. Consider a directed graph G=(V;A), where
Vis the set of all nodes and A=f(i;j)ji;j2V;i,jgis
the set of all arcs between each pair of nodes. The set V
include the pickup node set P=f1;:::; ng, the delivery node
setC=fn+1;:::; 2ng, the courier set K=f1;:::; kg, the set
of pickup nodes for couriers’ unﬁnished orders PU, and the
set of delivery nodes for couriers’ unﬁnished orders CU. An
order icorresponds to a pickup node iand a delivery node
i+n. The set of pickup nodes and delivery nodes is deﬁned
asN=P[C. The set of couriers Kincludes dedicated couriers
KDand crowdsourced couriers KG. Each courier k2Khas
an dummy original node o(k)and a dummy destination node
d(k). In each dispatch cycle, multiple orders can be assigned to
each courier. According to the courier’s experience level, there
is a maximum number of orders that can be simultaneously
served by courier k, denoted as Lk. The number of unﬁnished
orders (i.e., orders on hand) of courier kis denoted as hk.
The operational cost of the platform is composed of four
parts. For dedicated couriers, they can get reward for each
order [19]. The reward of crowdsourced couriers is directly
proportional to the delivery distance of orders, and can get 
reward per unit kilometer [2]. Furthermore, each crowdsourcedTABLE I
NOTATION
courier kaccepts the order iwith a probability ik, and the cost
coecient of rejection probability of crowdsourced couriers
isc. Besides, each order has a meal preparation completion
time biand a latest delivery time li. The service time of each
node is si. The distance between any two nodes is deﬁned as
di jand the travel time of any two nodes is deﬁned as ti j. If
the order is not delivered before the latest delivery time li,
we set the penalty cost per unit time of overtime as cd. The
objective of the proposed optimization problem is to minimize
the total costs, which consist of the labor costs of couriers,
rejection cost and overtime cost. The main notations are shown
in Table I.
Thus, the proposed problem is to determine the orders
assigned to each courier and the sequence of the nodes to
be visited by each courier so as to minimize the total costs for
the platform. The preference of each crowdsourced courier
ikis predicted through a data-driven approach (see section
IV), which is further incorporated in the optimization model.
Fig. 1 displays a feasible solution with 7 orders that are served
by 2 dedicated couriers and 1 crowdsourced courier. The two
dedicated couriers depart from the same dummy original node
to complete Orders 1, 2, and Orders 3, 4 respectively. The
crowdsourced courier needs to delivery Orders 5, 6, and 7.
Referring to the prior studies [1], [27], we make the
following assumptions about the problem:
1) All couriers with electric bicycles have a constant and
identical speed.

4) The individual order acceptance probability is indepen-
dent from each other. When the platform dispatch more
than one order to the courier at a time, we suppose the
orders are listed in the order of the priori probability,
which does not change the matching level between the
courier and the order.

the second item represents the cost of the crowdsourced
couriers, the third item represents the cost due to the potential
crowdsourced couriers’ orders rejection, and the fourth item
represents the penalty cost due to overtime.

Constraint (2)deﬁnes the overtime for each delivery node.
Constraints (3)and (4)indicate that each order is assigned
to a courier, where dedicated couriers can serve all orders,
while crowdsourced couriers can only serve a subset of orders.
Constraint (5)means that delivery nodes assigned to each
courier are directly served by the courier. Constraints (6)and
(7)indicate that each pickup node and delivery node can
only be visited by one courier once. Constraints (8)and(9)
specify that unﬁnished orders for couriers must be visited by
the couriers. Constraint (10) means that the dedicated couriers
and crowdsourced couriers need to start from the original node
and return to the destination node. Constraint (11) ensures the
ﬂow balance. Constraint (12) dictates that the same courier
must visit both the pickup and delivery nodes for the same
order. Constraint (13) states that if the courier visits the node
jimmediately after the node i, the arrival time at the node j
must be greater than or equal to the sum of the arrival time
at node i, the service time at node iand travel time between
the two nodes. Constraint (14) indicates that couriers must
ﬁrst visit the pickup node before visiting the delivery node.
Constraint (15) states that the time when the courier arrives at
the pickup node cannot be earlier than the meal preparation
time of the order. Constraint (16) speciﬁes the maximum order
capacity for a courier. Constraints (17) -(20) are variables’
domain constraints.
IV. S OLUTION METHODS
In this section, we propose a data-driven two-module
framework that synergistically integrates machine learning and
operations research techniques for the order assignment and
routing problem. As illustrated in Fig. 2, in the predictive
module, we employ XGBoost to predict the acceptance prob-
ability of each order for clustered crowdsourced couriers. In
the optimization module, these predicted probabilities are then
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:42 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
3448 IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON INTELLIGENT TRANSPORTATION SYSTEMS, VOL. 27, NO. 3, MARCH 2026
Fig. 2. The data-driven two-module framework.
incorporated into the optimization model, which is solved
using a logic-based Benders decomposition algorithm.
A. Predictive Model
In this section, a predictive model for crowdsourced
couriers’ order acceptance probability is developed. First, we
ﬁgure out eight features based on the dataset as preliminary
work for crowdsourced couriers’ clustering. After charactering
the crowdsourced couriers into groups, we apply four predic-
tive machine learning algorithms to predict the individual order
acceptance probability of each crowdsourced courier. The best
predictive algorithm is selected after performance evaluation.
1) Feature Engineering: In this section, we conduct fea-
ture engineering to ﬁgure out the crowdsourced couriers’
order preferences and characterize them into dierent groups.
According to the dataset, we design two categories (i.e. courier
features and order features) with a total of eight features to
describe crowdsourced couriers’ order acceptance behavior, as
shown in the Table II.
The methodology to quantify the courier features are
described as below. To determine the orders on hand (R1),
ﬁrstly, we identify all orders under the current courier ID.
Then, we ﬁlter out the orders whose dispatch time is earlier
than the current dispatch time and the delivery time is later
than the current dispatch time as R1.
For the following four features (i.e., R2, R3, R4 and
R5), the ﬁrst step is to obtain the restaurant locations and
customer locations corresponding to the courier’s orders on
hand (i.e., unﬁnished orders). By identifying the orders on
hand of the current courier ID, the corresponding restaurant
locations can be obtained from the restaurant’s latitude and
longitude accordingly. Similarly, the corresponding customer
locations can be obtained from the customer’s latitude and
longitude. Afterwards, we calculate the maximum distanceTABLE II
DESCRIPTION OFFEATURES
from the restaurant location of the current dispatched order
to those restaurant locations of the courier’s unﬁnished orders
(R2). Similarly, we can ﬁgure out the maximum distance
from the customer location of the current dispatched order
to all restaurant locations of the courier’s unﬁnished orders
(R3); the maximum distance from the restaurant location of
the current dispatched order to all customer locations of the
courier’s unﬁnished orders (R4); the maximum distance from
the customer location of the current dispatched order to all
customer locations of the courier’s unﬁnished orders (R5).
The methodology to quantify order features are shown
below. O1 is obtained by subtracting the dispatch time from
the estimated arrival time. O2 is obtained by subtracting the
platform order time from the estimated meal preparation time.
O3 is calculated based on the coordinates of the restaurant
and the customer, representing the Manhattan distance in a
two-dimensional plane.
Finally, outliers for the above eight characteristics are
identiﬁed and removed using the interquartile range method,
resulting in 4,881 couriers with a total of 298,211 data. An
outlier is the value which is statistical anomaly and signiﬁ-
cantly deviates from other observations.
2) Crowdsourced Couriers’ Order Preferences Clustering:
Due to the limited data for some crowdsourced couriers,
building a predictive model for each courier would result in
underﬁtting during the training process and lower prediction
accuracy. Hence, before predicting the individual probabil-
ity for each crowdsourced courier, we use the K-Means++
clustering method to characterize the crowdsourced couriers
into groups. By clustering couriers with similar behavioral
characteristics into groups for model training, the issue of
insucient courier data is addressed. For the selection of
initial cluster centers, the K-means++ algorithm improves on
the K-means algorithm’s random selection by employing a
probabilistic method. This algorithm ensures that the distances

SUN et al.: CROWDSOURCED DELIVERY PROBLEM WITH COURIERS’ PREFERENCES 3449
between cluster centers are as far as possible, reducing the risk
of getting stuck in local optimal solutions and achieving better
clustering results and faster convergence speed.

The clustering and prediction processes are shown in Algo-
rithm 1. The inputs are the features of the crowdsourced
couriers, the maximum number of clusters Lmaxand the max-
imum number of iterations Imax. In order to set a reasonable
upper limit Lmaxfor the number of clusters, we conduct
preliminary experiments. We ﬁnd that when Lmaxis set to 15 or
above, the clustering eect does not improve, but the model’s
training time increases a lot. Therefore, we set parameter Lmax
as 15. Finally, the model outputs the best number of clusters
L, best clustering result best clusters and best clustering model
best model.
3) Predictive Algorithms: To further predict the individual
order acceptance probability of each crowdsourced courier
who belongs to a speciﬁc cluster ﬁgured out in the previous
subsection, in this section, we introduce four predictive meth-
ods, including logistic regression (LR), random forest (RF),
XGBoost, and support vector machine (SVM) [18], [28]. The
explanation on how the four methods are applied to predict
the order acceptance probability is shown in Appendix VI of
Supplementary Material. We evaluate the performance of those
four machine learning techniques and choose the best one for
individual probability prediction.
B. Logic-Based Benders Decomposition Algorithm
The proposed problem consists the assignment of orders to
couriers and the sequencing of orders for each courier. The two
subproblems are interconnected, and the optimization method
to solve the proposed problem should deal with the sub-
problems properly [29]. The fundamental concept of Bendersdecomposition is to decompose the original problem into a
relaxed master problem and a linear subproblem [30]. After the
master problem passes the optimal solution to the subproblem,
the subproblem is solved continuously to generate Benders
cuts, which are added to the master problem. Furthermore,
the subproblem in this study can be further decomposed into
subproblems for each courier. Thus, the master problem and
the subproblem can interact iteratively, and the computational
burden is expected to be reduced. In our problem, the classical
Benders decomposition is not applicable because it requires
the subproblem to be a continuous linear programming prob-
lem. However, our subproblem involves an order sequencing
decision problem that includes integer decision variables.
Therefore, this paper employs a LBBD algorithm to solve the
subproblem with integer variables [31]. The LBBD derives
Benders cuts by solving a more general reasoning dual, rather
than applying linear programming duality to the subproblem as
in classical Benders decomposition. According to the problem
features of our study, the original problem is divided into a
master problem for order assignment and a subproblem for
order sequencing.
1) Master Problem: We introduce a continuous variable 
to represent the lower bound of the overtime penalty cost of
all orders. Therefore, the master problem can be expressed as
follows.

the minimum overtime penalty cost arising from the on-hand
orders of the courier.

5) Validity of the Proposed Benders Cut: This section
demonstrates the eectiveness of the proposed analytical Ben-
ders cuts. Referring to the existing literature [36], [37], an
eective cut must satisfy two conditions: (i) it must eliminate
suboptimal solutions generated by the current master problem;
(ii) it cannot eliminate any global optimal solutions. The
ﬁrst condition is straightforward. If the same set of orders
is assigned to the same courier in subsequent iterations, the
variable value of the overtime cost in the master problem
should at least become worse, so that the cut can eectively
remove the current solution. Otherwise, the assignment to the
courier must be altered. In any case, the solution at iteration h
will be excluded from the master problem space. The second
condition is somewhat more complex, and we provide the
following proof.
Theorem 1: Given iteration h, if the customer set assigned
to courier kisCh
k, the minimum overtime cost is h
k(Ch

customer iis removed from set Ch

not exceed cdmaxf0; 2tmax

depicts the routes of two couriers after they have removed
customers 2 and 4 respectively in iteration h. The black line
represents the optimal path of iteration h, the blue dotted line

6) Dynamic Programming Algorithm for Subproblems: To
minimize overtime costs, the subproblem deals with order
sequencing for each courier. It is found that solving the
subproblem is very time-consuming compared to the master
problem, which signiﬁcantly reduces the overall eciency
of the LBBD. Therefore, a speciﬁc dynamic programming
algorithm is proposed to solve the sorting problem of orders
for each courier, which improves algorithm eciency.
Algorithm 2 Dynamic programming for solving subproblems
Input Pickup nodes, customer nodes, couriers’ original position,
meal preparation time and latest delivery time of orders,
service time, and travel time matrix
Output Optimal route and minimum overtime duration
1Deﬁne the mapping function of the nodes
2Initialize dp(dynamic programming table)  1 and
parent (predecessor node) -1
3 For set of all possible accessed points S:
4 For all possible current nodes i:
5 Ifcurrent status is valid (i.e., dp,1):
6 For unvisited node j:
7 Ifnode jis a delivery node and the corre-
sponding pickup node is not accessed:
8 continue
9 Else:
10 Calculate the minimum overtime accord-
ing to the recursive equation (41)
11 Update dpandparent
12End for
13Return optimal route and minimum overtime
The recursive equation is shown in equation (41), where
f(C;) represents the minimum overtime when the order set
isC,tjrepresents actual arrival time of the order j, and lj
represents the latest delivery time of the order j. This recursiveequation is initialized to f(;;)=0, and the algorithm frame-
work of dynamic programming is shown in Algorithm 2below.
First, a mapping function between the original point set and
the newly numbered point set is established. Then, recursive
equation (41) is employed to calculate the shortest arrival
times and overtime from the original point to other points.
The algorithm gradually updates the arrival time and overtime
through the dynamic programming table (dp), ensuring the
constraints of pickup time windows and pickup priority are
satisﬁed. Finally, the optimal path and overtime are obtained
by backtracking through parent.

7) Branch-and-Check: The classical LBBD method solves
the master problem to optimality ﬁrst, followed by solving
subproblems to generate Benders cuts, which are then added
to the master problem for iterative solving. In reality, it has
been observed that the time taken to solve the master problem
to optimality increases as the continuous addition of Benders
cuts. In this section, we introduce the branch-and-check frame-
work which is an exact algorithm technique to enhance the
eectiveness and eciency of solving the proposed MILP
models. For solving complex master problems and simple
subproblems, it has been proven that branch-and-check is
an ecient method [37]. In branch-and-check, the master
problem is solved using the branch-and-cut method and is
solved only once. Each time the feasible solution of the master
problem is found, the subproblem is then solved. Benders
cuts are generated and added to each node of the master
problem search tree. Deﬁne zkas the upper bound found by the
subproblem in iteration k. If the lower bound vkfound by the
master problem is equal to zk, then a global optimal solution is
found. Otherwise, Benders cuts generated from subproblems
are added to the master problem to continue solving until the
globally feasible solution is optimal. The algorithm framework
of the whole LBBD is shown in Algorithm 3.
Algorithm 3 LBBD for the order assignment and routing
problem
1Initialization Cuts ;; zmin 1
2While vk,zminand the time limit is not exceeded do
3 Solve the master problem of order assignment in a
single search tree branch
4 Ifa feasible solution xk, objective value vkdo
5 Dynamic programming to solve the subproblems
given xk, the optimal value zk
6 Ifzk<zminthen
7 Update upper bound zmin zk
8 End
9 Generate Benders cuts
10 Update cut and add cuts to other nodes of the search
tree of the master problem
11 Repeat 2-10
12End while
13Return Optimal solution and optimal objective value

V. C OMPUTATIONAL EXPERIMENTS
In order to evaluate the performance of the proposed
algorithm, numerical experiments are conducted using real
data from Meituan to generate instances of various scales.
All models and algorithms are coded in Python 3.7 linked
with GUROBI 11.0.2. All the computational experiments are
conducted on a personal computer equipped with Intel(R)
Core(TM) i9-14900HX CPU (2.20 GHz) and 32 GB RAM.
The running time limit for all instances is set to 3600 seconds.
A. Data and Instance Generation
1) Data for the Predictive Model: The data comes from the
data-driven research competition jointly organized by Informs
and Meituan.6This data contains 654,343 order information
from Meituan between October 17, 2022 and October 24,
2022, involving 4954 couriers. The ﬁelds included in the data
are shown in Table IIIbelow.
The original data often has problems with data quality,
which are mainly shown in three aspects: (1) incompleteness,
where important ﬁelds have missing values; (2) inconsistency,
which is deﬁned by abnormal values; and (3) redundancy,
which involves duplicate records. Our study applies a stan-
dardized procedure to the data, which includes data cleaning
and data transformation, in order to improve data quality and
the reliability of the analytical results.
Data cleaning involved addressing outliers and missing
values in several ﬁelds. For the “platform order time” ﬁeld,
6Online available at https: //connect.informs.org/tsl/tslresources/
datachallenge (accessed on Jan2025).anomalous records with a value of 0 are removed. For time-
related ﬁelds, such as “estimate meal prepare time”, records
with null values are eliminated. The ﬁnal cleaned dataset
comprises 622,757 valid records from 4,938 riders, with a data
completeness rate of 95.17%.
Data transformation included the normalization of geo-
graphic coordinates and time ﬁelds. For the latitude and
longitude ﬁelds, a two-step conversion is applied: (1) For-
mat conversion: Integer-stored coordinates (e.g., 174579111,
45862631) are converted to decimal format (e.g., 174.579111,
45.862631); (2) Spatial mapping: Coordinates are projected
onto a two-dimensional plane using the Web Mercator
projection. For time-related ﬁelds, standardization involves
converting Unix timestamps into a standard date-time format
using the fromtimestamp function from Python’s datetime
module. For instance, the timestamp 1665977925 is trans-
formed into the format of “2022-10-17 11:38:45”.
2) Instance Generation for the Optimization Model: To
obtain the optimal solutions in a reasonable time, and con-
duct comparisons between Gurobi and the proposed LBBD
regarding the solution quality, we test small to medium size
instances (i.e., n=10, 20, 30, 40). In addition, we extract
the order information from the real dataset in the same time
slot (i.e., 1 minute) to form an instance. For example, we
extract 10 orders’ information in the same time slot to form an
instance with n=10. The number of couriers is determined by
the information in the dataset, where the number of dedicated
couriers and the number of crowdsourced couriers are set to
be 1:1. Five instances are generated for each scale. For each
instance, it is assumed that crowdsourced couriers can only
serve 50% of customers, while dedicated couriers can serve all
customers. The parameters for each instance are set as follows.
First, the longitude and latitude coordinates of merchants,
customers, and couriers in the dataset are transformed into
two-dimensional plane coordinates. Then we calculate the
distance of any two coordinates according to the Manhattan
distance. According to the speed limit of electric bicycle
(which is the main transport tool of couriers), we set the
courier’s driving speed as 25 km/h.7Besides, according to
the feedback from the couriers of Meituan and Ele.me plat-
forms, the delivery fee per order is around 4-6 RMB. The
delivery range is usually less than 3km. Therefore, we set
the delivery fee per order for a dedicated courier is 5 RMB.
For a crowdsourced couriers, the delivery fee per km is 1.5
RMB. Similarly, we set the unit overtime penalty cost and
the cost coecient of the rejection probability as 0.6 and 0.5,
respectively. We also conduct sensitivity analyses in terms of
dierent parameters such as delivery fee, penalty, to check the
robustness of the model’s performance in Section VPART
D. The meal preparation time and expected delivery time are
obtained from the variables “estimate meal prepare time” and
“estimate arrived time” respectively. The weight of each order
is randomly generated from the uniform distribution in U[1; 5].
The service time of each node is randomly generated from
the uniform distribution in U[10; 240]. The order capacity of
7Online available at https: //www.gov.cn/zhengce/202501/
content 6998231.htm (accessed on June 2025.

couriers is randomly generated from the uniform distribution
in U[2; 13] based on the existing orders on hand.
B. Results for the Predictive Model
In this section, we compare the dierences in predictive per-
formance among four methods. In addition, we also introduce
the tuning of XGBoost hyperparameters and explain the model
through feature importance ranking.
1) Performance Comparison of Predictive Models: The
predictive results of the four models are shown in Table IV.
Best L represents the optimal number of clusters. Average
AUC represents the average Area Under Curve of all clusters,
and is used to evaluate the performance of the classiﬁcation
model. The range of AUC values is from 0 to 1, with larger
values indicating stronger classiﬁcation ability. Average Recall
refers to the average recall rate of all clusters, which measures
the model’s ability to identify positive samples. Run time
represents the running time of the model. In the comparison
of four predictive models, it is found that XGBoost performed
the best, with the highest Average AUC (0.7446) and Average
Recall (0.8689). This indicates that 86.89% of the samples that
actually accepted are correctly predicted. And the AUC of RF
(0.7121) is second to XGBoost (0.7446). LR has the shortest
training time (33123s) and is suitable for quickly obtaining
model results, but its AUC (0.6273) and Recall (0.6253) are
relatively low. Although SVM has some classiﬁcation ability,
its training time is the longest. SVM performs poorly in both
performance and computational eciency. Overall, XGBoost
is the optimal choice. The XGBoost predictive model is
optimized through hyperparameter tuning, and the optimal
values of XGBoost hyperparameters are shown in Table AI
in Appendix I of Supplementary Material.
2) Interpretability of the Model: Feature importance analy-
sis provides a perspective for understanding the interpretability
of models. By analyzing feature importance, we can identify
which factors play a key role in the decision-making process.
In our study, the importance ranking of each feature in the
six categories derived from XGBoost is shown in Fig. A1 in
Appendix II of Supplementary Material. At the same time,
we aggregate the daily order acceptance rate of each courier
type aimed to analyze the relationship between the couriers’
preferences and their work experience. The aggregated data
are presented in Table AII in Appendix II of Supplementary
Material.
We can observe that the sixth category of couriers (Fig.
A1f) has the lowest average daily order acceptance rate, while
the ﬁrst ﬁve categories of couriers (Fig. A1a-1e) have daily
order acceptance rates above 80%. Consequently, we initially
categorize the six types of couriers into two major groupsbased on their job experience. For Fig. A1f, this group consists
of couriers with little job experience, for whom delivery
distance is a key factor. These couriers are unfamiliar with
the delivery environment and are more likely to accept small-
scale orders with concentrated geographical distribution.
For the ﬁrst ﬁve categories of couriers, the order acceptance
rate in Fig. A1a is relatively low. This category of couriers
demonstrates high autonomy and ﬂexibility in order accep-
tance. They pay more attention to timeliness and workload,
tending to optimize the eciency of current tasks.
Figure A1(b) depicts couriers who are extremely time-
sensitive. The remaining delivery time (O1) carries the highest
weight, followed by the number of orders on hand (R1). This
indicates that such couriers are highly sensitive to overtime
risks while also being mindful of their workload. They tend
to complete existing orders before accepting new ones.
Figure A1(c) illustrates dynamically coordinated couriers.
While the remaining delivery time (O1) and the number of
orders on hand (R1) remain dominant, the spatial feature
weights (R2, R3, R4, R5) are relatively balanced. This results
in a decision-making process that balances time, task load,
and spatial dimensions, demonstrating a global awareness of
the delivery network.
Figure A1 (d) presents time-conservative couriers. Here,
the remaining delivery time (O1) is the primary factor, while
the weights of the max distance from current customer to
unﬁnished restaurants (R3) and the max distance from current
restaurant to unﬁnished customers (R4) are comparable. This
suggests that such couriers prioritize time sensitivity over
spatial sensitivity and may be more willing to accept orders
with ﬂexible deadlines but longer distances.
Figure A1 (e) describes route-preferring couriers. The
weight of the max distance from current customer to unﬁn-
ished restaurants (R3) is notably high, while the max distance
from current restaurant to unﬁnished customers (R4) and the
max distance from current customer to unﬁnished customers
(R5) exhibit a synergistic eect. These couriers focus on
the spatial compatibility of new orders with their existing
route, making route convenience a key factor in their decision-
making. Therefore, when dispatching orders, the platform
should customize rules for dierent categories of couriers
based on their characteristics. For example, for couriers in Fig.
A1b, the platform needs to control order dispatch frequency
to fully leverage its eciency advantages, thereby enhancing
overall service quality. For groups that are more sensitive
to geographic distribution (e.g. Fig. A1f), optimizing the
range of order assignments can reduce long-distance delivery.
By thoroughly understanding the characteristics and deci-
sion factors of dierent categories of couriers, the platform
can enhance the accuracy and success rate of dispatching
orders, consequently improving overall delivery eciency.
This can also increase courier satisfaction and reduce occur-
rences like couriers rejecting orders due to inappropriate
dispatching.
C. Algorithmic Performance of LBBD Method
In this section, the proposed LBBD method is compared
with the commercial solver Gurobi in solving the proposed

MILP. The computational results of the two models are shown

As shown in Table V, the performance of LBBD regarding
the computational eciency and solution quality is signiﬁ-
cantly better than those of Gurobi. In the 20 instances, the
LBBD method solves all instances to optimality, while Gurobi
can only solve 15 instances to optimality. When the number
of orders is 30, the average gap of the MILP model solved
by Gurobi is 0.03%. When the number of orders is 40, the
average gap of the MILP model solved by Gurobi is 1.20%. In
terms of computational time, the LBBD method is much faster
than Gurobi. For all 20 instances, the average computation
time of the MILP model by Gurobi is 1166.02 seconds, about
12 times that of the LBBD method (94.54 seconds). It can
also be observed that as the number of orders increases,
the computational time for solving the original MILP model
is increased dramatically, while the LBBD method requires
much less computational time to solve the same instances.
For instances with 40 orders, the computational time achieved
by the proposed LBBD method is 268 seconds, much less
than that of Gurobi (i.e., 2115 seconds). In conclusion, LBBD
outperforms Gurobi in both solution quality and computational
eciency.

to compare the performance of the proposed methodology
and four heuristic rules. Due to the page limit, we put
the comparison analysis into Appendix III of Supplementary

COMPARISON OF THE PROPOSED NO-GOODCUTS AND ANALYTICAL CUTS
D. Algorithmic Performance of the Benders Cuts
Preliminary experiments have demonstrated that the no-
good cuts have limited eects on the convergence of the
algorithm and solution quality. These cuts reduce the solution
eciency and make it harder to decrease the search space.
Therefore, in order to increase the constraints of the cuts
on our problem to improve the algorithmic performance, we
further propose the new analytical Benders cuts. Based on the
structural analysis of the problem, the analytical Benders cuts
are designed to more eectively capture the key constraints,
thereby shrinking the feasible solution space and accelerating
convergence in each iteration. To evaluate the performance
of the newly proposed analytical Benders cuts, we utilize 20
instances with orders of 10; 20;30;40 for experiments. We
use both the no-good cuts and the analytical Benders cuts,
and compare the computational results of the two kinds of
cuts. The results are shown in Table VI. Additionally, several
indicators are detailed in the table, including the number of
iterations (#Iter) and the number of Benders cuts added to the
master problem (#Cuts). From this table, it can be observed
that both cuts perform well in the LBBD method proposed
in this section. However, the performance of the analytical
Benders cuts is still superior to that of the no-good cuts, in
terms of computational time. The average computation time
for the analytical Benders cut is 85.60s, which is about 81%
faster than the no-good cut (443.69s). The average number of
iterations and cuts for the analytical Benders cut are 477.75
and 19607.10, respectively (both lower than the no-good cut).
The computational results verify that the analytical Benders
cut is more ecient than the no-good cut.
E. Sensitivity Analysis
In this section, we further investigate the impacts of dierent
cost parameters in the objective function on the optimal
dispatch solutions through sensitivity analysis. We conduct
the sensitivity analysis over problem scales of n=10, 20,
30 and 40. The total cost of the platform is composed of

1) Fees Paid to Dedicated Couriers for Each Order Deliv-

results presented in Table VII. Note that when is changing,
the proposed LBBD method obtain optimal solutions for
all instances. First, as the delivery payment for dedicated
couriers gradually increases, there is a noticeable decline in
the orders allocated to dedicated couriers, contrasted by an
increase in orders assigned to crowdsourced couriers. How-
ever, it is observed that the penalty costs for late delivery
is also increased a bit, which is caused by the increase of
orders allocated to crowdsourced couriers. This is because
there are orders on hand by the crowdsourced couriers that
may incur overtime delivery for new assigned orders. It
indicates that, although it may reduce the service quality by
transforming some orders from the dedicated couriers to the
crowdsourced couriers, it is a trade-o solution among the
multiple performance criteria. If the total number of dedicated
couriers becomes smaller and the payment have to be added,
it is much wiser for platform to hire more crowdsourced
couriers while considering their preferences at the same
time.

crowdsourced couriers decrease and the orders allocated to
dedicated couriers increase. This indicates that the system
tends to prioritize dedicated couriers with relatively low cost
to control the overall cost. When =2, the dedicated couriers
take at least 60% of the orders. And the overtime decreases,
reﬂecting the improvement of the dedicated couriers’ delivery
eciency. Moreover, with the increase of , fewer and fewer
orders are allocated to crowdsourced couriers, and the cost
due to the order rejection by crowdsourced couriers also
decreases.
3) Cost Coecient of the Rejection Probability: Assuming
the cost coe cient of the rejection probability varies within
the rangef3;4;5;6g, the upper limit for the latest deliv-
ery time of orders is set at 1800 seconds. Here, we relax
the original assumption and let the customer set served by
the crowdsourced couriers identical to that of the dedicated
couriers. The other parameters remain consistent with those
outlined in part A of Section V. Subsequently, various cost
values are recorded, along with the overtime duration for the
dedicated couriers, as presented in Table IX. It is evident
that the number of orders allocated to dedicated couriers is
gradually increasing, while those assigned to crowdsourced

couriers are decreasing. This indicates that when the platform
is reluctant to allocate orders to crowdsourced couriers to avoid
low delivery eciency due to a high order rejection rate, i.e.,
a high cost coecient for the rejection rate, the platform will
force the dedicated couriers to deliver these orders, resulting in
an imbalance in order dispatch. Furthermore, the overtime for
orders delivered by dedicated couriers tends to increase, which
may reduce customer satisfaction signiﬁcantly. It is indicated
that, for the platform, it is wiser to hire more crowdsourced
couriers to improve delivery eciency. However, if the plat-
form becomes too “risk averse” to the order rejection rate by
crowdsourced couriers, it may inhibit the order assignment to
the crowdsourced couriers and reduce the delivery system’s
eciency.
4) Penalty Cost Coecient of Unit Overtime: We set the
penalty cost coecient for late delivery varies within the
rangef1;2;3;4g, the upper limit for the latest delivery time
of orders is set as 1800 seconds. Here, we ﬁx the cost
coecient of the order rejection rate as 5. We again relax
the original assumption and let the customer set that can be
served by the crowdsourced couriers is identical to that of the
dedicated couriers. Other parameters remain consistent with
those outlined in part A of Section V. The values of the
various costs and the number of orders assigned to dierent
types of couriers are presented in Table X. As the tardiness
penalty cost coecient cdincreases from 1 to 4, the total
cost TCalso increases progressively, while the total overtime
decreases. The number of orders allocated to dedicated couri-
ers shows a downward trend, whereas the cost associated with
crowdsourced couriers shows an upward trend. This indicates
that even the platform sets a high cost coecient regarding
order rejection rate to avoid re-dispatching, it is still wiser to
allocate more orders to crowdsourced couriers to guarantee
on-time delivery of orders and minimize total delivery delays,
by considering the couriers’ preference and controlling the
potential risk that the crowdsourced couriers choose to reject
servicing the assigned orders. In addition, it is found that the
cost of potential order rejection does not increase too much.
Therefore, it is identiﬁed that the proposed model helps the
platform to realize trade-o between the total cost, service
quality and the crowdsourced couriers’ welfare.TABLE XI

ORDER ACCEPTANCE PREFERENCE
Besides the aforementioned sensitivity analysis, to evaluate
the impact of the prediction error on the optimal solution, we
also conduct a sensitivity analysis to observe the variance in
the objective values and allocation results. Due to the page
limit, we put this part into Appendix V of Supplementary
Material.
F . Importance of Considering Couriers’ Order Preferences
In this section, we analyze the importance of considering
couriers’ order preferences during the order dispatching deci-
sion making process. For the optimal solution obtained by
the proposed model, the sum of the payment for dedicated
couriers DC 0, crowdsourced couriers CC 0and the overtime
penalty OC 0is represented by Z0, and the cost of potential
rejected orders by the crowdsourced couriers is represented
byRC0. At the same time, we consider the benchmark where
couriers’ order preference is not considered, and the sum
of the three costs, i.e. the payments of dedicated couriers
DC 1, crowdsourced couriers CC 1, and overtime cost OC 1, is
expressed in RC1, and the cost of rejected orders under the
benchmark is expressed as RC1. For an in-depth analysis, we
select 15 cases with the number of orders n=f10;20;30;40g.
In these instances, the upper limit of latest delivery time is set
to 1800s, and the cost coecient of the potential number of
rejected orders is set into a moderate level, i.e., =3. Other
parameters are the same as those in part A of Section V.
The results are presented in Table XI, which illustrates
the cost dierences between the proposed model and the
benchmark. We can ﬁnd that the cost due to potential rejected
orders under the benchmark is nearly twice of that of the pro-
posed model (i.e., considering the couriers’ order preference),
while the total operational cost increased under the proposed
model is lower than 1%. This indicates that incorporating
the individual preferences of crowdsourced couriers into the
model does not lead to notable operational cost increase,
instead, it signiﬁcantly increases the dispatching reliability by
improving the couriers’ willingness to accepting the orders
Authorized licensed use limited to: Mukesh Patel School of Technology & Engineering. Downloaded on July 06,2026 at 19:24:42 UTC from IEEE Xplore.  Restrictions apply. 
SUN et al.: CROWDSOURCED DELIVERY PROBLEM WITH COURIERS’ PREFERENCES 3457
assigned to them. Furthermore, the model that accounts for
courier preferences not only reduces the total cost but also
enhances couriers’ welfare, which is crucial for improving
delivery eciency and service quality. Although in certain
speciﬁc cases, the impact of couriers’ preferences on costs
may not be obvious. But in general, the model based on
couriers’ order preferences demonstrates a clear advantage
in optimizing couriers dispatch, reducing operational costs,
and enhancing couriers’ welfare. Therefore, for on-demand
delivery platforms, integrating courier preference informa-
tion into the courier dispatch system is a vital strategy
for achieving more e cient and cost-eective operations,
as well as an important pathway to societal sustainable
operations.
VI. C ONCLUSION
To solve the problem of inadequate consideration of courier
behavior characteristics in traditional on-demand delivery
courier dispatch system, our research proposes a novel data-
driven method framework that incorporates predictive analysis
into the mathematical optimization to address the uncer-
tainty of crowdsourced couriers’ willingness to accept orders
in the on-demand delivery dispatch system. Based on the
large-scale order data from the Meituan platform, this study
identiﬁes and extracts eight features that inﬂuence couriers’
order acceptance behavior, and applies an XGBoost model for
order acceptance probability prediction. Afterwords, a mixed
integer programming model is formulated to optimize the
order dispatching decisions by incorporating the predicted
probability of accepting speciﬁc orders to the speciﬁc couriers,
which is further solved by the LBBD algorithm. An e-
cient analytical cut is built to accelerate the computational
time for optimality. Numerical experiments show the substan-
tial beneﬁts of this proposed model in enhancing dispatch
system’s eciency, reducing rejection rates, and increasing
the crowdsourced couriers’ welfare. In addition to providing
a novel order dispatching strategy for on-demand delivery
platforms, this study oers new insights on courier behavior
characteristics.
This study has limitations, and can be extended for future
work. First, to increase the accuracy and ﬂexibility of courier
dispatching strategies, it is essential to consider the integra-
tion of real-time information (e.g. weather, trac situation)
to construct an adaptive dynamic predictive model that can
continuously update courier preferences. To be speciﬁc, the
posterior probability when more than one orders dispatched at
a time can be further analyzed in the future. Second, to model
the delivery time more accurately, the assumptions could be
further relaxed. For example, the varying speeds of individual
couriers and more accurate calculation of the distance between
two nodes (rather than Manhattan distance) based on the
information of map service provides (e.g. AutoNavi API)
will be considered. Third, the exploration of more advanced
artiﬁcial intelligence algorithms, such as reinforcement learn-
ing, deep reinforcement learning, or optimization algorithms
based on graph neural networks, could be beneﬁcial. These
methodologies have the potential to signiﬁcantly improve
computational eciency and dispatch eectiveness in complexscenarios through continuous learning and large-scale data
training.
